--- Page 7 ---
<meta>This is the title page of Francis Hutcheson's influential work on aesthetics and ethics. The handwritten inscriptions indicate this specific copy belonged to John Adams, the second President of the United States, acquired in 1773.</meta>

<insert>John Adams 1773.</insert>

#### AN
# INQUIRY
#### INTO THE
### ORIGIN of our IDEAS
#### OF
<insert>Adams 2918</insert>
## BEAUTY and VIRTUE;
### In Two TREATISES.

#### I. Concerning BEAUTY, ORDER, HARMONY, and DESIGN.
#### II. Concerning MORAL GOOD and EVIL.

---

->The Second Edition, Corrected and Enlarged.<-

---

> Therefore, of those very things which are perceived by sight, no other animal perceives beauty, grace, or the symmetry of parts. Nature and reason, transferring this analogy from the eyes to the mind, think that beauty, consistency, and order should be even more strictly maintained in our decisions and actions. From these elements is forged and fashioned that "moral goodness" <note>original: "honestum"</note> which we seek: which, even if it were not celebrated, would still be honorable; and which, even if it were praised by no one, is by its very nature praiseworthy. You see the very form and, as it were, the face of moral goodness; if it could be seen with the eyes, it would awaken a marvelous love for wisdom. <note>original Latin: "Itaque eorum ipsorum quæ aspectu sentiuntur... mirabiles amores excitaret sapientiæ." Quoted from Cicero, *On Duties* (De Officiis), Book 1, Chapter 4.</note>
<insert>2576</insert>

---

->**L O N D O N:**<-

->Printed for **J. DARBY**, **A. BETTESWORTH**, **F. FAYRAM**, **J. PEMBERTON**, **C. RIVINGTON**, **J. HOOKE**, **F. CLAY**, **J. BATLEY**, and **E. SYMON.** 1726.<-

<header>DEDICATION.</header>

What I was unwilling to claim as my own work, I did not dare presume to dedicate to any person of great reputation.

**YOUR** Excellency’s favorable reception of these writings soon relieved me of all fears regarding how they would be received by the wiser and better part of the world. Since this has given me the confidence to acknowledge them as my own, I humbly venture to dedicate them in this second edition to your Excellency. By doing so, I may have the opportunity to express my sincerest gratitude for the notice you were pleased to take of me, while also having the pleasure of letting the

<summary>The author explains that the positive reception of the first edition by the addressee gave them the confidence to claim the work and dedicate this second edition to them.</summary>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page begins the preface, following the dedication to Lord Carteret.</meta>

## THE
# PREFACE.

There is no part of philosophy of more importance than a correct knowledge of human nature and its various powers and dispositions. Our recent inquiries have been very much focused on our understanding and the various methods of obtaining truth. We generally acknowledge that the importance of any truth is nothing other than its significance, or effectiveness in making people happy, or giving them the greatest and most lasting pleasure; and wisdom denotes only a capacity for pursuing this end by the best means. It must surely then be of the greatest importance,

<meta>catchword: to</meta>

<summary>Hutcheson argues that understanding human nature is the most important part of philosophy, as the value of truth is measured by its ability to produce happiness.</summary>
<keywords>philosophy, human nature, truth, wisdom, happiness, pleasure, understanding</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the sentence from the previous page regarding the importance of understanding the end goal of human action and the nature of happiness.</meta>

<header>The PREFACE.</header>

*...to have distinct conceptions of this end itself, as well as of the means necessary to obtain it; so that we may find out which are the greatest and most lasting pleasures, and not employ our reason, after all our laborious improvements of it, in trifling pursuits. It is to be feared, indeed, that most of our studies, without this inquiry, will be of very little use to us; for they seem to have scarcely any other tendency than to lead us into speculative knowledge itself. Nor are we clearly told how it is that knowledge or truth is pleasant to us.*

**THIS** *consideration led the author of the following papers to inquire into the various pleasures which human nature is capable of receiving. We shall generally find in our modern philosophical writings nothing further on this topic than some bare division of them into "sensible" and "rational," and some trite*

<meta>catchword: Com-</meta>

<summary>Hutcheson argues that without understanding which pleasures are most lasting, human reason and study are wasted on trifles. This motivated him to investigate the various pleasures of human nature, which he believes are poorly explained in contemporary philosophical works.</summary>
<keywords>human nature, pleasures, reason, speculative knowledge, philosophical writings, truth, happiness</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues the sentence from the previous page, finishing the phrase "some trite commonplace arguments."</meta>

<header>The PREFACE.</header>

*Commonplace arguments to prove the latter more valuable than the former. Our sensible pleasures are slightly passed over, and explained only by some instances in tastes, smells, sounds, or the like, which men of any tolerable reflection generally look upon as very trifling satisfactions. Our rational pleasures have had much the same kind of treatment. We are seldom taught any other notion of rational pleasure than that which we have upon reflecting on our possession, or claim to those objects, which may be occasions of pleasure. Such objects we call advantageous; but advantage, or interest, cannot be distinctly conceived, until we know what those pleasures are which advantageous objects are apt to excite, and what senses or powers of perception we have with respect to such objects. We may perhaps find such an inquiry of more importance in morals, to prove what we call the reality of virtue, or*

<summary>Hutcheson critiques the superficial way philosophers distinguish between sensible and rational pleasures. He argues that "advantage" and "interest" cannot be understood until we identify the specific pleasures and perceptive powers they involve, a task vital for establishing the reality of virtue.</summary>

<keywords>sensible pleasures, rational pleasures, advantage, interest, morals, reality of virtue, perception, human nature</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the sentence from the previous page, finishing the phrase "to prove what we call the reality of virtue, or".</meta>

<header>The PREFACE.</header>

*that it is the agent's surest happiness, more than one would at first imagine.*

IN *reflecting on our external senses, we plainly see that our perceptions of pleasure or pain do not depend directly on our will. Objects do not please us according to how we incline they should. The presence of some objects necessarily pleases us, and the presence of others just as necessarily displeases us. Nor can we, by our will, obtain pleasure or avoid pain in any other way than by obtaining the former kind of objects and avoiding the latter. By the very framework of our nature, the one is made the occasion for delight, and the other for dissatisfaction.*

THE *same observation will hold for all our other pleasures and pains. For there are many other sorts of objects which please or displease us just as necessarily as material objects*

<summary>The author argues that human perceptions of pleasure and pain are involuntary and determined by our natural constitution rather than our will. This necessity applies to all forms of pleasure and pain, whether they arise from material objects or other sources.</summary>

<keywords>external senses, perception, pleasure, pain, will, human nature, involuntary response, sensory objects</keywords>

HIS principal *Design* is to show,
" *That Human Nature was not left*

" *Virtue, to form to itself Obser-*

" *accordingly to regulate its Con-*

<summary>The author defines the "Internal Sense" as the ability to perceive beauty and order, and the "Moral Sense" as the natural inclination to find pleasure in virtuous actions. He argues that humans are naturally predisposed toward virtue because human reason is too weak and distracted to rely solely on calculating the advantages of conduct.</summary>
<keywords>Observation, Internal Sense, Moral Sense, Beauty, Virtue, Human Nature, Reason, Affections, moral philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 21 ---
<meta>This page continues the sentence from page xiv, completing the argument that human reason alone is insufficient to guide moral conduct due to the complexity of calculating long-term advantages.</meta>

<page-num>xv</page-num>
<header>The PREFACE.</header>

*long deductions of reason, which show some actions to be, on the whole, advantageous to the agent, and their contraries pernicious. The* AUTHOR *of Nature has furnished us much better for virtuous conduct than our* moralists *seem to imagine, by almost as quick and powerful instructions as we have for the preservation of our bodies. He has made virtue a lovely form to excite our pursuit of it; and has given us strong affections to be the springs of every virtuous action.*

*THIS moral sense of beauty in actions and affections may appear strange at first view. Some of our* moralists *themselves are offended at it in my* LORD SHAFTESBURY; *so much are they accustomed to deduce every approbation or aversion from rational views of interest (except where it concerns the simple ideas of the external senses), and they have such a horror of innate ideas,*

<summary>The author argues that God has equipped humans with a "moral sense" and natural affections to guide virtuous behavior, rather than forcing us to rely on slow logical deductions. He notes that many contemporary moralists reject this concept of an inherent moral sense because they prefer to explain all human behavior through calculated self-interest and a rejection of innate ideas.</summary>
<keywords>Author of Nature, Virtue, Moralists, Lord Shaftesbury, Moral Sense, Self-Interest, Innate Ideas, Affections</keywords>

--- Page 22 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument regarding the "moral sense," addressing the common fear that such a concept implies a belief in "innate ideas."</meta>

<page-num>xvi</page-num>
<header>The PREFACE.</header>

*which they imagine this borders upon. But this moral sense has no relation to innate ideas, as will appear in the second treatise. Our gentlemen of good taste can tell us of a great many senses, tastes, and relishes for beauty, harmony, and imitation in painting and poetry; and may we not also find in mankind a relish for beauty in characters and in manners? I suspect we have made philosophy, as well as religion—by our foolish management of it—so austere and ungainly a form that a gentleman cannot easily bring himself to like it; and those who are strangers to it can scarcely bear to hear our description of it. It has changed so much from what was once the delight of the finest gentlemen among the ancients, and their recreation after the bustle of public affairs!*

IN *the first treatise, the author perhaps in some instances has gone too far, in supposing a greater agree-*

<sig>3</sig>
<meta>catchword: ment</meta>

<summary>The author clarifies that the "moral sense" is distinct from the controversial concept of "innate ideas" and compares it to the aesthetic appreciation of art and poetry. He laments that modern philosophy and religion have become so rigid and unattractive compared to the revitalizing role they played for the ancient Greeks and Romans.</summary>
<keywords>moral sense, innate ideas, beauty, aesthetics, philosophy, religion, the ancients, manners, characters</keywords>

> *"That there is some*
> *"sense of beauty natural to men;*
> *"that we find as great an agree-*
> *"ment among men in their tastes for*
> *"forms as in their external senses,*
> *"which everyone agrees are natural;*
> *"and that pleasure or pain, de-*
> *"light or aversion, are naturally*
> *"joined to their perceptions."*

If the reader is convinced of such inclinations of the mind to be pleased with forms, proportions, resemblances, and theorems, it will be no difficult matter to understand another superior sense, also natural to men, which inclines them to be pleased with actions, characters, and affections. This is the moral sense, which is the subject of the second treatise.

**THE** proper occasions of perception by the external senses occur to us as soon as we come into the

<summary>The author explains that while we recognize physical senses as natural because they appear at birth, we often mistakenly attribute our senses of beauty and morality to education because they take longer to manifest. He argues that the mind is naturally designed to find pleasure in good character and proportion just as it is in sound or taste.</summary>

sation, and is still fond of expressing his grateful remembrance of him; but,

->*Id cinerem, & Manes credas curare sepultos.*<-

*To be involved with this book can be no honor to a person so justly celebrated for the most generous sentiments of virtue and religion, delivered with the most manly eloquence; yet it would not be just toward the world should the author conceal his obligations to the Reverend Mr. Edward Synge, not only for revising these papers when they stood in great need of an accurate review, but for suggesting several just amendments in the general scheme of morality. The author was much confirmed in his opinion of the correctness of these thoughts upon finding that this gentleman had fallen into the same way of thinking before him; and will ever look upon his friendship*

<sig>3</sig>
<meta>catchword: as</meta>

<summary>The author concludes his remembrance of the late Lord Molesworth and acknowledges the Reverend Mr. Edward Synge for his essential editorial revisions and contributions to the work's moral philosophy.</summary>

### SECT. I.
->*Concerning some Powers of Perception, distinct from what is generally understood by Sensation.*<-

To make the following observations understood, it may be necessary to premise some *definitions* and *observations*, either universally acknowledged or sufficiently proved by many writers, both ancient and modern, concerning our perceptions called *sensations* and the actions of the mind following them.

*Art.* I. **Those** ideas which are raised in *sensation*, the mind upon the presence of external ob-

jects,

--- Page 36 ---
<meta>This page continues the first section of the inquiry, elaborating on the nature of sensations and the active and passive powers of the mind in processing perceptions.</meta>

<header>An INQUIRY concerning</header>
<sig>2</sig>

Sect. 1. jects, and their acting upon our bodies, are called *sensations*. We find that the mind in such cases is passive, and does not have the power directly to prevent the perception or idea, or to vary it at its reception, as long as we continue our bodies in a state fit to be acted upon by the external object.

<margin>Different senses.</margin>
II. WHEN two perceptions are entirely different from each other, or agree in nothing but the general idea of *sensation*, we call the powers of receiving those different perceptions, *different senses*. Thus *seeing* and *hearing* denote the different powers of receiving the ideas of colors and sounds. And although colors have vast differences among themselves, as sounds also do; yet there is a greater agreement among the most opposite colors than between any color and a sound: hence we call all colors perceptions of the same sense. All the various senses seem to have their distinct organs, except *feeling*, which is in some degree diffused over the whole body.

<margin>How the mind is active.</margin>
III. THE mind has a power of compounding ideas which were received separately; of comparing their objects by means of the ideas, and of observing their *relations* and *proportions*; of enlarging and diminishing its ideas at pleasure, or in any certain *ratio* or *degree*; and of considering separately

<margin>Substances.</margin>
IV. THE ideas of *substances* are compounded of the various simple ideas jointly impressed when they presented themselves to our senses. We define substances only by enumerating these sensible ideas; and such definitions may raise an idea clear enough of the substance in the mind of one who never immediately perceived the substance, provided he has separately received by his senses all the simple ideas which are in the composition of the complex one of the substance defined. But if there are any simple ideas which he has not received, or if he lacks any of the senses necessary for the perception of them, no definition can raise any simple idea which has not been previously perceived by the *senses*.

VI. MANY of our sensory perceptions are immediately pleasant, and many painful, and that without any knowledge of the cause of this pleasure or pain, or how the objects excite it, or are the occasions of it; or without seeing what further advantage or detriment the use of such objects might lead to. Nor would the most accurate knowledge of these things change either the pleasure or pain of the perception, however much it might give a rational pleasure distinct from the sensory one; or might raise a distinct joy from a prospect of further advantage in the object, or aversion from an apprehension of evil.

<margin>Sect. 1.</margin>
<margin>Complex Ideas.</margin>
VIII. THE only pleasure of sense which our philosophers seem to consider is that which accompanies the simple ideas of sensation: but there are vastly greater pleasures in those complex ideas of objects which obtain the names of *beautiful, regular, harmonious.* Thus, everyone acknowledges he is more delighted with a fine face or an accurate picture than with the view of any single color, even if it were as strong and lively as possible; and more pleased with a prospect of the sun rising among settled clouds and coloring their edges, with a starry hemisphere, a fine landscape, or a regular building, than with a clear blue sky, a smooth sea, or a large open plain that is not diversified by woods, hills, waters, and buildings: and yet even these latter appearances are not quite *simple*. So in music, the pleasure of fine *composition* is incomparably greater than that of any single note, no matter how sweet, full, or swelling it may be.

<margin>Beauty.</margin>
<margin>Harmony.</margin>
IX. LET it be observed that in the following papers, the word *beauty* is taken for the *idea raised in us*, and a *sense of beauty* for *our power of receiving this idea*. *Harmony* also denotes *our pleasant ideas arising from the composition of sounds*, and a *good ear* (as it is generally understood) denotes a *power of perceiving this pleasure*. In the following sections, an attempt is made

<margin>Internal Sense.</margin>
X. IT is of no consequence whether we call these ideas of *Beauty* and *Harmony* perceptions of the *External Senses* of seeing and hearing, or not. I should rather choose to call our power of perceiving these ideas an INTERNAL SENSE, if only for the convenience of distinguishing them from other sensations of seeing and hearing which men may have without a perception of *beauty* and *harmony*. It is plain from experience that many men have, in the common meaning, the senses of seeing and hearing perfectly enough; they perceive all the *simple ideas* separately and have their pleasures; they distinguish them from each other, such as one color from another—either quite different, or the stronger or fainter of the same color—when they are placed beside each other, although they may often confuse their *names* when they occur apart from each other, as some do the names of *green* and *blue*. They can tell in separate notes the *higher*, *lower*, *sharper*, or *flatter* when separately sounded; in figures they discern the *length*, *breadth*, and *width* of each line, surface, and angle; and may be as capable of hearing and seeing at great distances as any men

<summary>The author proposes the term "internal sense" to describe the specific power of perceiving beauty and harmony, distinguishing it from the basic external senses of sight and hearing. He notes that individuals can possess perfect physical senses and distinguish basic qualities like color or pitch without necessarily experiencing the pleasure of beauty.</summary>

This greater capacity for receiving such pleasant ideas we commonly call a *fine genius* or *taste*. In music, we seem universally to acknowledge something like a sense distinct from the external sense of hearing, and we call it a *good ear*; and we would probably acknowledge a similar distinction in other objects, if we also had distinct names to denote these *powers* of perception.

<margin>Different from the external sense.</margin>
XI. There will perhaps appear another reason later for calling this power of perceiving the ideas of *beauty* an *internal sense*: because in some other matters, where our *external senses* are not much involved, we discern a sort of beauty very similar, in many respects, to that observed in physical objects, and accompanied by a similar pleasure. Such is the beauty perceived in *theorems* or universal truths, in *general causes*, and in some *extensive principles of action*.

# An INQUIRY concerning

Sect. 1. of *natural Beauty*, which ravishes us even in
<image-desc>decorative horizontal wavy flourish</image-desc> his description; from that cold, lifeless *conception* which we imagine in a dull *critic*, or one of the *virtuosi* <note>Individuals with a deep interest in the arts and sciences, often focused on technical details or collecting.</note>, without what we call a fine *taste*. This latter class of men may have greater perfection in that knowledge which is derived from external sensation; they can tell all the *specific differences* of *trees*, *herbs*, *minerals*, and *metals*; they know the *form* of every *leaf, stalk, root, flower,* and *seed* of all the *species*, about which the *poet* is often very ignorant. And yet the *poet* shall have a vastly more delightful perception of the *whole*; and not only the *poet* but any man of a fine *taste*. Our external senses may, by measuring, teach us all the proportions of *architecture* to the tenth of an *inch*, and the position of every *muscle* in the human body; and a good *memory* may retain these. Yet there is still something further necessary, not only to make a man a complete master in *architecture, painting,* or *statuary*, but even a tolerable judge of these *works*, or capable of receiving the highest *pleasure* in contemplating them. Since, then, there are such different *powers of perception* where what are commonly called the *external senses* are the same; since the most accurate *knowledge* of what the *external senses* discover often does not give the *pleasure* of *beauty* or *harmony*, which yet one of a good *taste* will en-

He notes that while a critic or scientist may understand physical proportions and classifications, only the internal sense allows one to enjoy the "whole" as a poet does.</summary>

The difference of the perceptions seems sufficient to justify the use of a different name, especially when we are told in what meaning the word is applied.

<margin>Its pleasures are necessary and immediate.</margin>
XIII. THIS superior power of perception is justly called a *sense* because of its affinity to the other senses in this: that the pleasure does not arise from any *knowledge* of principles, proportions, causes, or of the usefulness of the object, but strikes us at first with the idea of beauty. Nor does the most accurate *knowledge* increase this pleasure of beauty, however it may super-add a distinct rational pleasure from prospects of *advantage* or from the increase of knowledge <note>See above, Art. 6.</note>.

XIV. AND further, the ideas of beauty and harmony, like other sensible ideas, are necessarily pleasant to us, as well as immediately so; neither can any resolution of our own, nor any *prospect* of advantage or disadvantage, vary the beauty or deformity of an object. For as in the external sensations, no view of *interest* will

Similarly, if one were to propose the whole world as a *reward*, or threaten the greatest evil, to make us approve a deformed object or disapprove a beautiful one, dissimulation may be procured by rewards or threats; or we may in our external conduct abstain from any *pursuit* of the beautiful and pursue the deformed. However, our *sentiments* of the forms and our *perceptions* would continue invariably the same.

<margin>*This sense is prior to and distinct from prospects of self-interest.*</margin>

XV. HENCE it plainly appears "that some objects are immediately the occasions of this pleasure of beauty, and that we have senses fitted for perceiving it; and that it is distinct from that *joy* which arises from self-love upon the prospect of *advantage*." Indeed, do we not often see convenience and use neglected to obtain beauty, without any other prospect of *advantage* in the beautiful form than the suggestion of pleasant ideas of beauty? Now this shows us that however we may pursue beautiful objects from self-love, with a view to obtain the pleasures of beauty—as in architecture, gardening, and many other matters—yet there must be a *sense* of beauty prior to prospects even of this advantage. Without this sense, these objects would not be thus

Our *sense* of *beauty* from objects, by which they are made good to us, is very distinct from our *desire* for them when they are thus constituted: Our *desire* for beauty may be counterbalanced by rewards or threats, but never our *sense* of it; even as fear of death, or love of life, may make us choose and desire a bitter potion, or neglect those foods which the *sense* of taste would recommend as pleasant; and yet no prospect of advantage, or fear of evil, can make that potion agreeable to the *sense*, or food disagreeable to it, which was not so prior to this prospect. Just in the same manner regarding the *sense* of *beauty* and *harmony*; that the *pursuit* of such objects is frequently neglected, due to prospects of advantage, aversion to labor, or any other motive of self-love, does not prove that we have no *sense* of beauty, but only that our *desire* for it may be counterbalanced by a stronger *desire*: So gold outweighing silver is never cited as proof that the latter lacks gravity.

<margin>Beauty: Original or Comparative.</margin>
XVII. **BEAUTY** is either *original* or *comparative*; or, if some prefer the terms, *absolute* or *relative*: However, let it be observed that by *absolute* or *original* beauty, we do not mean any quality supposed to exist in the object that would be beautiful in itself, without relation to any mind that perceives it: For beauty, like other names of sensible ideas, properly denotes the perception of a mind; just as *cold, hot, sweet,* and *bitter* denote sensations in our minds that perhaps have no resemblance to the objects that excite these ideas in us, even though we generally imagine that there is something in the object exactly like our perception. We, therefore, by \* *absolute* beauty, understand only that beauty which we perceive in *objects* without *comparison* to anything external of which the *object* is supposed an *imitation* or *picture*; such as that beauty perceived from the *works of nature*, *artificial forms*, *figures*, and *theorems*. *Comparative* or *relative* beauty is that which we perceive in *objects* commonly considered as *imitations* or *resemblances* of something else. These two kinds of beauty employ the three following *sections*.

## SECT. II.

### *Of Original or Absolute Beauty.*

<margin>*The Sense of Men.*</margin>
I. SINCE it is certain that we have *ideas* of beauty and harmony, let us examine what *quality* in objects excites these *ideas*, or is the cause of them. And let it be observed here that our inquiry is only about the *qualities* which are beautiful to *men*, or about the foundation of their *sense* of beauty; for, as was hinted above, beauty always has a relation to the *sense* of some mind. When we later show how generally the objects we encounter are *beautiful*, we mean that such objects are agreeable to the *sense* of *men*. For just as there are many objects which seem in no way beautiful to *men*, we also see a variety of other *animals* who seem delighted with them; they may have *senses* constituted differently than those of *men*, and may have *ideas* of beauty excited by objects of a completely different *form*. We see animals suited for every place; and what appears to *men* as crude and shapeless, or loathsome, may be a *paradise* to them.

II. SO that we may more clearly discover the general foundation or cause of

# An INQUIRY concerning

### Sect. 2.

figure, or the circle to which regular polygons have an obvious relation, is so much lost to our observation that beauty does not always increase with the number of sides; and the lack of parallelism in the sides of heptagons and other figures of odd numbers may also diminish their beauty. So in solids, the *icosahedron* surpasses the *dodecahedron*, and this the *octahedron*, which is still more beautiful than the *cube*; and this again surpasses the regular *pyramid*. The obvious basis for this is greater *variety* with equal *uniformity*.

<margin>Uniformity.</margin>
**THE** greater *uniformity* increases *beauty* amidst equal *variety* in these instances: an *equilateral triangle*, or even an *isosceles*, surpasses the *scalene* triangle. A *square* surpasses the *rhombus* or *lozenge*, and this again the *rhomboid*, which is still more beautiful than the *trapezium* or any *figure* with irregular curved *sides*. So the *regular solids* vastly surpass all other *solids* of an equal number of flat *surfaces*. The same is observable not only in the *five* perfectly regular *solids*, but in all those which have any considerable *uniformity*, such as *cylinders*, *prisms*, *pyramids*, and *obelisks*; these please every *eye* more than any crude *figures* where there is no *unity* or *resemblance* among the *parts*.

<summary>The author explains that beauty in geometric shapes arises from a balance of variety and uniformity, noting that regular solids and symmetrical polygons are more aesthetically pleasing than irregular or crude forms. He specifically compares various shapes, such as the icosahedron and the square, to demonstrate how increased complexity (variety) and consistency (uniformity) enhance beauty.</summary>

# BEAUTY, ORDER, etc. Sect. 2.

<margin>Compound ratio.</margin>
INSTANCES of the compound *ratio* are found in comparing *circles* or *spheres* with *ellipses* or *spheroids* that are not very eccentric; and in comparing the compound *solids*, the *exoctahedron* and *icosidodecahedron*, with the perfectly regular ones from which they are compounded. We shall find that the lack of that most perfect *uniformity* observable in the latter is compensated by the greater *variety* in the others, so that the *beauty* is nearly equal.

IV. THESE *observations* would probably hold true for the most part and might be confirmed by the *judgment* of *children* regarding *simpler figures*, where the *variety* is not too great for their *comprehension*. And however uncertain some of the aforementioned particular *instances* may seem, this is nonetheless perpetually to be observed: that *children* are fond of all *regular figures* in their little *diversions*, although they are no more convenient or useful to them than the *shapes* of our common *pebbles*. We see how early they discover a *taste* or *sense* of *beauty* in desiring to see *buildings*, regular *gardens*, or even *representations* of them in *pictures* of any kind.

<margin>Beauty of Nature.</margin>
V. IT is the same *foundation* which we have for our *sense* of *beauty* in the *works* of NATURE. In every *part* of the *world*

# An INQUIRY concerning

## Sect. 2.
Which we call *beautiful*, there is a vast *uniformity* amidst an almost infinite *variety*.
Many parts of the universe seem not at all designed for the use of man; indeed, it is but a very small spot with which we have any acquaintance. The *figures* and *motions* of the great bodies are not obvious to our senses, but found out by *reasoning* and *reflection* upon many long observations: and yet as far as we can by *sense* discover, or by *reasoning* enlarge our knowledge and extend our imagination, we generally find their structure, order, and motion agreeable to our sense of *beauty*. Every particular object in *nature* does not indeed appear *beautiful* to us; but there is a vast profusion of *beauty* over most of the objects which occur either to our senses or reasonings upon observation: For not to mention the apparent situation of the heavenly bodies in the circumference of a great sphere, which is wholly occasioned by the imperfection of our sight in discerning distances; the forms of all the great bodies in the universe are nearly *spherical*; the orbits of their revolutions generally *elliptical*, and without great eccentricity, in those which continually occur to our observation: now these are figures of great uniformity, and therefore pleasing to us.

BEAUTY, ORDER, &c. Sect. 2.
C 3

FURTHER, to pass by the less obvious *uniformity* in the *proportion* of their *quantities* of *matter*, *distances*, and *times* of revolution to each other; what can exhibit a greater instance of *uniformity* amidst *variety* than the constant tenor of *revolutions* in nearly equal *times* for each *planet* around its *axis* and the central fire or *SUN*, through all the ages of which we have any *records*, and in nearly the same *orbit*? By which, after certain *periods*, all the same *appearances* are again renewed; the alternate successions of *light* and *shade*, or *day* and *night*, constantly pursuing each other around each *planet*, with an agreeable and regular *diversity* in the *times* they possess the several *hemispheres* in the *summer*, *harvest*, *winter*, and *spring*; and the various *phases*, *aspects*, and *situations* of the *planets* to each other, their *conjunctions* and *oppositions*, in which they suddenly darken each other with their conical shades in *eclipses*, are repeated to us at their fixed periods with invariable constancy: these are the *beauties* which charm the *astronomer* and make his tedious *calculations* pleasant.

VI. AGAIN, regarding the dry part of the surface of our globe, a great part of which is covered with a very pleasant, inoffensive color, how beautifully is it diversified with various degrees of *light* and *shade*, according to the different positions of the parts of its surface in *mountains*, *valleys*, *hills*, and open *plains*, which are variously inclined toward the great LUMINARY!

<margin>Plants.</margin>

VII. IF we descend to the smaller works of NATURE, what vast *uniformity* exists among all the species of *plants* and *vegetables* in the manner of their growth and propagation! What exact resemblance exists among all the plants of the same species, whose numbers surpass our imagination! And this *uniformity* is not only observable in the overall form; indeed, in this it is not so very exact in all instances, but in the structure of their smallest parts, which no eye unassisted by lenses can discern. In the almost infinite multitude of *leaves*, *fruit*, *seeds*, and *flowers* of any one species, we often see an exact *uniformity* in the structure and arrangement of the smallest fibers. This is the *beauty* which charms an ingenious *botanist*. Indeed, what vast *uniformity* and regularity of shape is found in each particular *plant*, *leaf*, or *flower*! In all *trees* and most of the smaller plants, the stalks or trunks are either nearly *cylinders* or regular *prisms*;

There is a further beauty arising from that figure, which is a natural indication of *strength*; but this may be passed over, because it may probably be alleged that our approval of this shape flows from an opinion of its *advantage*, and not from the form itself.

The beauty arising from *mechanism*, apparently adapted to the *necessities* and *advantages* of any animal—which pleases us even though there is no advantage to ourselves following from it—will be considered under the head of *Relative Beauty*, or *Design* *.

<margin>Birds.</margin>
XI. The peculiar beauty of *birds* can scarcely be omitted; it arises from the vast variety of feathers, which are a curious sort of machines adapted to many admirable uses. These feathers retain a vast resemblance in their structure among all species, and a perfect *uniformity* in those of the same species in the corresponding parts, and on the two sides of each individual. This is in addition to all the beauty of lively colors and gradual shades, seen not only in the external appearance of the bird—resulting from an artful combination of shaded feathers—but often visible even in a single feather separately.

<margin>Fluids.</margin>
XII. If our reasonings about the *nature* of *fluids* are sound, the vast stores of *water* will provide us an instance of *uniformity* in nature beyond imagination, when we reflect upon the almost infinite multitude of small, polished, smooth spheres which must be supposed to be formed in all the parts of this globe. The same *uniformity* probably exists among the parts of other *fluids* as well as water; and the same must be observed in several other natural bodies, such as salts, sulphurs, and the like, whose uniform properties probably depend upon a *uniformity* in the shapes of their parts.

<margin>Harmony.</margin>
XIII. Under *original beauty* we may include *harmony*, or *beauty of sound*—if that expression may be allowed—because *harmony* is not usually conceived as an imitation of anything else. *Harmony* often raises pleasure in those who do not know what the cause of it is; and yet the foundation of this pleasure is known to be a sort of *uniformity*. When the several vibrations of one note regularly coincide with the vibrations of another, they make an agreeable composition, and such notes are called *concords*. Thus, the vibrations of any one note coincide in *time* with two vibrations of its *octave*, and two vibrations of any note coincide with three of its *fifth*, and so on for the rest of the *concords*.

<sig>3</sig>
<meta>catchword: cords.</meta>

<vocab>Uniformity, Fluids, Harmony, Beauty of Sound, Concords, Vibrations, Octave, Fifth</vocab>

<summary>The author explains that the beauty of fluids and music arises from hidden uniformity, such as the uniform spherical particles in water or the mathematical coincidence of vibrations in musical concords.</summary>
<keywords>uniformity, fluids, harmony, beauty of sound, concords, vibrations, octave, physics, acoustics</keywords>

<page-num>28</page-num>
<header>An INQUIRY concerning</header>
<meta>catchword: But</meta>
<sig>XIV.</sig>

<margin>Sect. 2.</margin> concords. Now no *composition* can be harmonious in which the *notes* are not, for the most part, arranged according to these natural *proportions*. Besides which, a due *regard* must be had to the *key*, which governs the whole, and to the *time* and *mood* in which the *composition* is begun: a frequent and unskilled *change* of any of these will produce the greatest and most unnatural *discord*. This will appear by observing the *dissonance* which would arise from joining parts of different tunes together as one, even though both were separately agreeable. A similar *uniformity* is also observable among the *basses*, *tenors*, and *trebles* of the same tune.

There is indeed observable, in the best *compositions*, a mysterious effect of *discords*: they often give as much pleasure as continued *harmony*, whether by refreshing the ear with *variety*, or by awakening the attention and enlivening the *relish* for the succeeding *harmony* of *concords*—as *shadows* enliven and beautify *paintings*—or by some other means not yet known. Certain it is, however, that they have their place and some good effect in our best *compositions*. Some other powers of *music* may be considered hereafter*.

XIV. <margin>Sect. 2.</margin> **B**ut in all these instances of *beauty*, let it be observed that the pleasure is communicated to those who have never reflected on this general foundation; and that all being alleged here is this:

“sensation arises only from objects in

“*riety*.” We may have the sensation without knowing what is the cause of it, just as a man's *taste* may suggest ideas of sweets, acids, or bitters, even though he is ignorant of the *forms* of the small *bodies*, or their *motions*, which excite these perceptions in him.

<header>Sect. 3. An INQUIRY concerning</header>

# S E C T. III.

## Of the Beauty of Theorems.

<margin>Theorems. I.</margin>
The beauty of *theorems*, or universal truths demonstrated, deserves a distinct consideration, being of a nature quite different from the former kinds of *beauty*; and yet there is none in which we shall see such an amazing *variety* with *uniformity*: and from this arises a very great pleasure, distinct from any prospects of further advantage.

II. For in one *theorem* we may find included, with the most exact agreement, an infinite multitude of particular truths; indeed, often an infinity of infinities: so that although the necessity of forming abstract ideas and universal *theorems* arises perhaps from the limitation of our minds, which cannot admit an infinite multitude of singular ideas or judgments at once, yet this power gives us evidence of the largeness of the human capacity beyond our imagination. Thus, for instance, the 47th Proposition of the first *book* of Euclid’s *Elements* contains an infinite multitude of truths concerning the infinite possible sizes of right-angled triangles; as you make the area greater

<summary>The author examines the beauty of mathematical theorems, arguing that they provide a unique pleasure by encompassing an infinite variety of specific truths within a single uniform statement. He suggests that while abstract theorems help overcome the limitations of the human mind, they also demonstrate its remarkable capacity for universal understanding.</summary>
<keywords>theorems, mathematical beauty, uniformity, variety, Euclid’s Elements, geometry, abstract ideas, human capacity</keywords>

<page-num>31</page-num>
<header>BEAUTY, ORDER, &c.</header>

or less; and in each of these *sizes* you may <margin>Sect. 3.</margin> find an infinite multitude of dissimilar *triangles*, as you vary the proportion of the *base* to the *perpendicular*; all these infinities of infinities agree in the general theorem. In *algebraic* and *fluxional* <note>Referring to the method of fluxions, an early form of calculus developed by Isaac Newton.</note> *calculations*, we shall still find a greater *variety* of particular *truths* included in general *theorems*; not only in general *equations* applicable to all kinds of *quantity*, but in more particular *investigations of areas and tangents*: in which one manner of operation shall discover *theorems* applicable to infinite orders or *species of curves*, to the infinite *sizes* of each *species*, and to the infinite *points* of the infinite *individuals* of each size.

<margin>Foundation of their beauty.</margin>
III. **T**hat we may the better discern this agreement, or *unity* of an infinity of objects in the general *theorem*, to be the foundation of the *beauty* or *pleasure* attending their discovery, let us compare our satisfaction in such discoveries with the uneasy state of mind we are in when we can only measure lines or surfaces by a scale, or are making experiments which we cannot reduce to any general *canon*, but are only heaping up a multitude of particular incoherent observations. Now each of these trials discovers a new truth, but with no *pleasure* or *beauty*, notwithstanding—

<summary>The author explains that the beauty of mathematical theorems arises from their ability to unify infinite particular truths within a single general principle. He contrasts the intellectual pleasure of discovering these universal laws with the tedious and unsatisfying task of collecting isolated, disconnected observations.</summary>

IV. AGAIN, let us take a metaphysical axiom, such as this: *Every whole is greater than its part*; and we shall find no *beauty* in the contemplation. For though this proposition contains many infinities of particular truths; yet the *unity* is inconsiderable, since they all agree only in a vague, undetermined conception of *whole* and *part*, and in an indefinite excess of the former over the latter, which is sometimes great and sometimes small. So, should we hear that the *cylinder* is greater than the inscribed *sphere*, and this again greater than the *cone* of the same altitude and diameter as the base, we shall find no pleasure in this knowledge of a general relation of greater and less, without any precise difference or proportion. But when we see the universal, exact agreement of all possible sizes of such systems of solids, that they preserve to each other the constant *ratio* of 3, 2, 1; how beautiful is the *theorem*, and how are we ravished by its first discovery!

In the search of *nature*, there is a similar *beauty* in the knowledge of some great *principles*, or universal *forces*, from which innumerable *effects* flow. It is easy to see how men are charmed with the *beauty* of such *knowledge*, besides its *usefulness*; and how this motivates them to deduce the *properties* of each *figure* from one *genesis*, and to demonstrate the *mechanical forces* from one *theorem* of the *composition of motion*, even after they have sufficient knowledge and certainty of all these *truths* from distinct, independent *demonstrations*. <margin>Fantastic Beauty.</margin>
IT is no less easy to see what absurd attempts men have been led into by this sense of *beauty*, and a silly affectation of obtaining it in other sciences as well as in mathematics. It was this, probably, which set DESCARTES on that hopeful project of deducing all human knowledge from one proposition, namely, *Cogito, ergo sum* <note>I think, therefore I am</note>; while others with as little sense contended that *Impossibile est idem simul esse & non esse* <note>It is impossible for the same thing to be and not to be at the same time</note> had much fairer pretensions to the style and title of *Principium humanae Cognitionis absolute primum* <note>The absolutely first principle of human knowledge</note>. Mr. LEIBNITZ had an equal affection for his favorite principle of a *sufficient reason* for everything in nature, and brags to Dr. CLARKE of the wonders he had wrought in the intellectual world by its assistance; but his learned antagonist seems to think he had not sufficient reason for his boasting*. If we look into particular sciences, we may see in the systems learned men have given us of them,

VI. THIS delight which accompanies sciences, or universal *theorems*, may really be called a kind of *sensation*; since it necessarily accompanies the discovery of any proposition, and is distinct from bare knowledge itself, being most intense at first, whereas the knowledge is uniformly the same. And however knowledge enlarges the mind, and makes us more capable of comprehensive views and projects in some kinds of business, from which *advantage* may also arise to us; yet we may leave it to every student's own mind to determine whether he has not often felt this pleasure without any such prospect of advantage from the discovery of his *theorem*. All that can be inferred from this is only this: that as in our external senses, so in our *internal* ones, the pleasant sensations generally arise from those objects which calm reason

<margin>Works of Art.</margin>
VIII. As for the works of **ART**, if we were to examine various artificial contrivances or structures, we would constantly find that the foundation of the *beauty* which appears in them is some kind of *uniformity*, or *unity* of proportion among the parts, and of each part to the whole. Since there is a vast diversity of possible proportions and different kinds of *uniformity*, there is plenty of room for that diversity of *fancies* observed in *architecture*, *gardening*, and similar arts across different *nations*. They all may possess *uniformity*, though the parts in one may differ from those in another. *Chinese* or *Persian* buildings are not like *Grecian* and *Roman* ones, and yet the former has its own *uniformity* of various parts to each other and to the whole, just as the latter does. In the kind of architecture that **EUROPEANS** call *regular*, the *uniformity* of parts is very obvious; the several parts are regular *figures*, and are either *equal* or at least *similar* within the same range. The pedestals are *parallelepipeds* or square *prisms*; the pillars are nearly *cylinders*; the arches are *circular*, and all those in the same row are *equal*. The same proportion is observed everywhere in the same range between the *diameters* of *pillars* and their heights, their *capitals*, the *dia-*

And though other *countries* do not follow the *Grecian* or *Roman* proportions; yet there is even among them a proportion retained, a *uniformity*, and a resemblance of corresponding figures; and every deviation in one part from that proportion which is observed in the rest of the building, is displeasing to every eye, and destroys or at least diminishes the *beauty* of the whole.

IX. THE same might be observed through all other works of *art*, even to the humblest *utensil*; we shall always find the *beauty* of every one of these to have the same foundation of *uniformity amidst variety*, without which they appear lowly, irregular, and deformed.

### SECT. IV.

#### Of Relative or Comparative Beauty.

<margin>Comparative Beauty.</margin>
IF the preceding thoughts concerning the foundation of *absolute beauty* are just, we may easily understand what *relative beauty* consists of. All *beauty* is relative to the sense of some mind perceiving it; but what we call *relative* is that which is apprehended in any *object*, commonly considered as an *imitation* of some *original*: And this *beauty* is founded on a *conformity*, or a kind of *unity* between the *original* and the *copy*. The *original* may be either some *object* in nature, or some *established idea*; for if there is any known *idea* as a standard, and rules to fix this *image* or *idea* by, we may make a beautiful *imitation*. Thus a sculptor, painter, or poet, may please us with a HERCULES, if his piece retains that grandeur, and those marks of *strength*, and *courage*, which we imagine in that hero.

AND further, to obtain *comparative beauty* alone, it is not necessary that there be any beauty in the original; the imitation of *absolute beauty* may indeed on the whole make a more lovely piece, and yet

<meta>catchword: an</meta>

<summary>In Section 4, Hutcheson defines relative or comparative beauty as the beauty found in an imitation, arising from the conformity or unity between the copy and its original. He explains that even if an original object is not beautiful, its imitation can still possess comparative beauty through accurate representation.</summary>
<keywords>absolute beauty, relative beauty, comparative beauty, imitation, original, conformity, unity, aesthetics, Hercules</keywords>

--- Page 75 ---
<meta>This page continues Section 4, further exploring the concept of relative or comparative beauty as it applies to imitations of imperfect originals and the art of poetry.</meta>

<page-num>41</page-num>
<header>Beauty, Order, &c.</header>

an exact imitation shall still be *beautiful*, <margin>Sect. 4.</margin>
though the original were entirely void of it: <note>flourish</note>
thus the *deformities* of old age in a picture, or the *rudest* rocks or *mountains* in a *landscape*, if well represented, shall have abundant *beauty*, though perhaps not so great as if the original were *absolutely* beautiful, and as well represented.

<margin>Description in poetry.</margin>
II. **The** same observation holds true in the descriptions of the poets, either of *natural* objects or persons; and this *relative beauty* is what they should principally endeavor to obtain, as the peculiar *beauty* of their works. By the *Moratæ Fabulæ*, or the ἤθη of **Aristotle**, we are not to understand *virtuous manners* in a moral sense, but a *just representation* of manners or characters as they are in *nature*; and that the *actions* and *sentiments* be suited to the characters of the *persons* to whom they are ascribed in *epic* and *dramatic* poetry. Perhaps very good reasons may be suggested from the nature of our *passions* to prove that a poet should not draw his *characters* perfectly *virtuous*; these characters, indeed, abstractly considered, might give more pleasure and have more beauty than the imperfect ones which occur in life with a mixture of good and evil: but it may suffice at present to suggest against this choice that we have more lively ideas of *imperfect men* with all their passions,

<meta>catchword: than</meta>

<summary>Hutcheson explains that imitations of unappealing or "deformed" objects can still be beautiful through accurate representation. He applies this to poetry, arguing that "relative beauty" arises from the realistic portrayal of human characters and passions rather than the depiction of flawless virtue.</summary>
<keywords>relative beauty, imitation, landscape, poetry, Aristotle, Moratæ Fabulæ, characters, passions, realism, aesthetics</keywords>

--- Page 76 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of relative beauty in poetry, specifically focusing on why imperfect characters are more compelling and how poetic devices like metaphors function through resemblance.</meta>

<page-num>42</page-num>
<header>An INQUIRY concerning</header>

Sect. 4. than of *morally perfect heroes*, such as really never occur in our observation; and of which, consequently, we cannot judge exactly regarding their agreement with the original. And further, through consciousness of our own state, we are more nearly touched and affected by *imperfect characters*; since in them we see represented, in the persons of others, the *contrasts* of inclinations, and the *struggles* between the passions of *self-love* and those of *honor* and *virtue*, which we often feel in our own breasts. This is the perfection of *beauty* for which **Homer** is justly admired, as well as for the *variety* of his *characters*.

<margin>
Probability, simile, metaphor.
</margin>

III. **Many** other beauties of poetry may be reduced under this class of *relative beauty*: *Probability* is absolutely necessary to make us imagine *resemblance*; it is by *resemblance* that *similes*, *metaphors*, and *allegories* are made beautiful, whether either the subject or the thing compared to it have *beauty* or not; indeed, the *beauty* is greater when both have some original *beauty* or *dignity* as well as *resemblance*: and this is the foundation of the rule of studying *decency* in *metaphors* and *similes* as well as *likeness*. The *measures* and *cadence* are instances of *harmony*, and come under the head of *absolute beauty*.

<meta>catchword: IV. WE</meta>

<summary>Hutcheson argues that imperfect characters are more effective in literature because they resonate with our own internal struggles, and he explains that poetic beauty often arises from the resemblance found in metaphors and allegories.</summary>

<margin>
Proneness to compare.
</margin>

IV. We may here observe a strange *proneness* in our minds to make *perpetual comparisons* of all things which occur to our *observation*, even those which would seem very remote. There are certain *resemblances* in the *motions* of all *animals* upon similar *passions*, which easily establish a *comparison*; but this does not serve alone to entertain our *fancy*: *inanimate objects* often have such *positions* as resemble those of the *human body* in various *circumstances*; these *airs* or *gestures* of the *body* are *indications* of certain *dispositions* in the *mind*, so that our very *passions* and *affections*, as well as other *circumstances*, obtain a *resemblance* to *natural inanimate objects*. Thus, a *tempest* at *sea* is often an *emblem* of *wrath*; a *plant* or *tree* drooping under the *rain*, of a *person* in *sorrow*; a *poppy* bending its *stalk*, or a *flower* withering when cut by the *plow*, resembles the *death* of a *blooming hero*; an *aged oak* in the *mountains* shall represent an *old empire*, a *flame* seizing a *wood* shall represent a *war*. In short, everything in *nature*, by our strange inclination to *resemblance*, shall be brought to represent other things, even the most remote, especially the *passions* and *circumstances* of *human nature* in which we are more nearly concerned; and to confirm this, and furnish instances of it, one need

<margin>Intention.</margin>
V. **C**ONCERNING that kind of *comparative Beauty* which has a *necessary relation* to some *established Idea*, we may *observe* that some *Works* of *Art* acquire a *distinct Beauty* by their *correspondence* to some *universally supposed Intention* in the *Artificer*, or the *persons* who *employed* him. And to obtain this *Beauty*, sometimes they do not form their *works* so as to attain the *highest perfection* of *original Beauty* separately *considered*; because a *composition* of this *relative Beauty*, along with some *degree* of the *original kind*, may give more *pleasure* than a more *perfect original Beauty* separately. Thus we see that *strict regularity* in laying out *gardens* in *parterres*, *vistas*, and *parallel walks* is often *neglected* to obtain an *imitation* of *nature* even in some of its *wildness*. And we are more *pleased* with this *imitation*, especially when the *scene* is large and spacious, than with the more *confined exactness* of *regular works*. So likewise in the *monuments* erected in honor of deceased *heroes*, although a *cylinder*, or *prism*, or *regular solid* may have more *original Beauty* than a very acute *pyramid* or *obelisk*,

<summary>Hutcheson explains that some art derives beauty from its correspondence to an intended purpose rather than just original regularity, using examples like naturalistic gardens and commemorative monuments.</summary>

This may be the reason, too, why *columns* or *pillars* look best when made a little tapered from the middle, or a third from the bottom, so that they may not seem top-heavy and in danger of falling.

VI. A similar reason may influence artists in many other instances to depart from the rules of *original beauty*, as laid down above. And yet this is no argument against our sense of *beauty* being founded, as was explained above, on *uniformity amid variety*; it is only evidence that our sense of *beauty* of the *original kind* may be varied and overbalanced by another kind of *beauty*.

VII. This *beauty* arising from correspondence to *intention* would open to curious observers a new scene of *beauty* in the works of NATURE, by considering how the *mechanism* of the various parts known to us seems adapted to the perfection of that part, and yet remains in subordination to the good of some *system* or *whole*. # SECT. V.

## *Concerning our Reasonings about Design and Wisdom in the Cause, from the Beauty or Regularity of Effects.*

<margin>*Sense is arbitrary in its Author.*</margin>
I. THERE seems to be no necessary connection of our pleasing ideas of *beauty* with the *uniformity* or *regularity* of objects, based on the *nature* of things, prior to some *constitution* of the AUTHOR of our nature, which has made such forms pleasant to us. Other *minds* may be so framed as to receive no pleasure from *uniformity*; and we actually find that the same regular forms do not seem to please all the animals known to us equally, as shall probably appear afterwards. Therefore, let us make the most unfavorable supposition to the present argument, *namely*: that the constitution of our sense, so as to approve of *uniformity*, is merely arbitrary in the AUTHOR of our nature; and that there are an infinity of *tastes* or *relishings* of *beauty* possible; so that it would be impossible to throw together fifty or a hundred pebbles which would not make an agreeable habitation for some animal or other, and appear beautiful to it. And then it is

<meta>catchword: plain,</meta>

<summary>Hutcheson argues that our appreciation for uniformity and beauty is not an inherent necessity of nature but a specific constitution established by the Creator. He suggests that different beings might have entirely different senses of beauty, yet uses this challenging premise to begin an argument for design.</summary>
<keywords>beauty, uniformity, Author of Nature, design, perception, teleology, sense of beauty, aesthetics</keywords>

--- Page 82 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument from the previous page, moving from the possibility of arbitrary tastes in beauty to a mathematical argument against chance and for design in the creation of a world that suits the senses of its inhabitants.</meta>

<page-num>48</page-num>
<header>An INQUIRY concerning</header>

Sect. 5. plain, that from the perception of *beauty* in any one *effect*, we should have no reason to conclude *design* in the *cause*: for a *sense* might be so constituted as to be pleased with such *irregularity* as may be the effect of an *undirected force*. <note>* By *undirected force*, or *undesigning force*, is to be understood that *force* with which an agent may put matter into motion without having any design or intention to produce any particular form. *This "conatus ad motum" (effort toward motion), without an actual line of direction, seems such a gross absurdity in the Cartesian scheme that it is below the dignity of common sense to vouchsafe to confute it. But men have so many confused notions of some "nature" or "chance" impressing motions without any design or intention of producing any particular effect, that it may be useful to show that even this very absurd postulate, though it were granted to them, is insufficient to answer the appearances in the regularity of the world; and this is what is attempted in the first fourteen articles of this section. These arguments would really be useless if all men were persuaded of what to a man of just thought will appear pretty obvious: that there can be no thoughtless agent, and that chance and nature are mere empty names, as they are used on this occasion, relative only to our ignorance.*</note> But then, as there are an infinity of *forms* possible into which any *system* may be reduced, an infinity of *places* in which animals may be situated, and an infinity of *relishes* or *senses* in these animals is supposed possible; that in the immense spaces any one animal should by chance be placed in a *system* agreeable to its *taste*, must be improbable as *infinite to one* at least: And much more unreasonable is it to expect from *chance* that a multitude of *animals* agreeing in their *sense of beauty* should obtain agreeable *places*.

<sig>II. It</sig>

<summary>Hutcheson argues that while a sense of beauty could theoretically be pleased by random irregularity, the mathematical probability of animals being placed by chance into environments that perfectly match their specific senses is essentially zero. He further critiques the Cartesian concept of undirected motion, asserting that "chance" and "nature" are often just terms used to mask human ignorance of a thinking agent.</summary>
<keywords>beauty, design, cause, effect, undirected force, Cartesian scheme, conatus ad motum, chance, probability, aesthetics, teleology</keywords>

<page-num>49</page-num>
<header>BEAUTY, ORDER, etc.</header>
<meta>Section: Sect. 5.; Catchword: WE</meta>
<sig>E</sig>

<margin>
Undirected Force.
</margin>

II. **There** is also the same probability that in any one system of matter, an *undirected force* will produce a *regular form* as any one given irregular one of the same degree of complexity. But still, the irregular forms into which any system may be arranged surpass the *regular* ones in number, as *infinity* surpasses *unity*; for what holds in *one* small system will hold in a *thousand*, a *million*, or a *universe* with more advantage—namely, that the possible irregular forms infinitely surpass the *regular* ones. For instance, the *area* of an *inch square* is capable of an *infinity* of regular forms, such as the *equilateral triangle*, the *square*, the *pentagon*, *hexagon*, *heptagon*, etc. But for every single regular form, there are an infinite number of irregular ones, such as an infinity of *scalena* for the one equilateral triangle, an infinity of *trapezia* for the one *square*, of irregular *pentagons* for the one *regular* one, and so on. Therefore, assuming any one system is agitated by *undesigning force*, it is infinitely more probable that it will resolve itself into an *irregular form* than a *regular* one. Thus, the chance that a system of *six parts* upon agitation shall not take the form of a *regular hexagon* is at least *infinite* to *unity*; and the more complex we make the system, the greater the hazard, for a very obvious reason.

<summary>The author argues that while an undirected force could theoretically produce a regular form, the mathematical probability of it doing so is nearly zero because irregular forms infinitely outnumber regular ones. Using geometric examples, he demonstrates that as a system's complexity increases, the likelihood of achieving order by chance becomes even more improbable.</summary>
<keywords>probability, undirected force, regular forms, geometry, mathematical design, complexity, chance vs design</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the argument from the previous page, shifting from the mathematical probability of chance to our experiential reasoning that regularity implies design, while questioning whether this assumption holds if the designer has a different sense of beauty or none at all.</meta>

Sect. 5.

WE see this confirmed by our constant experience: that *regularity* never arises from any *undesigned force* of ours. From this, we conclude that wherever there is any *regularity* in the arrangement of a system capable of many other arrangements, there must have been *design* in the *cause*; and the strength of this evidence increases according to the *multiplicity* of parts employed.

BUT this conclusion is too hasty unless some further proof is introduced; and what leads us into it is this: Humans, who have a sense of *beauty* in *regularity*, are generally led in all their arrangements of bodies to seek some kind of *regularity* and seldom ever design *irregularity*. Hence, we judge the same of other beings too—namely, that they seek *regularity*. We presume *intention* in the *cause* wherever we see it, making *irregularity* always a presumption of a lack of *design*. Whereas if other agents have different senses of beauty, or if they have no sense of it at all, *irregularity* may as well be designed as *regularity*. And then let it be observed that in this case, there is just the same reason to conclude *design* in the *cause* from any one irregular effect as from a regular one; for since there are an infinity of other *forms* possible as well as this irregular

VI. AND let it be observed here that there are many compositions of bodies which the smallest degree of *design* could easily achieve, but which we would nonetheless expect in vain from all the powers of chance or undesigned force, even after an infinity of encounters; even supposing a dissolution of every form except for the *regular one*, so that the parts might be prepared for a new agitation. Thus, suppose we could expect *one* equilateral *prism* of any given dimensions to be formed from *undirected* force, within an infinity of matter that is somehow determined to resolve itself into bodies of a *given* solid content (which is all we could expect, since it is an *infinite to one* chance, after the *solid content* is obtained, that the body shall not be *prismatic*; and even if it is *prismatic*, it is *infinite to one* that it shall not be *equilateral*). *Infinite* and no more; the various *degrees* of force may make another power of *infinite*, and the number of *encounters* may make the fifth. But this last point only holds on the supposition that after every *encounter* there is no *cohesion*, but everything is dissolved again for a new gathering, except in *similar forms* or *exact combinations*; which supposition is entirely groundless, since we see *dissimilar bodies* cohering as strongly as any others, and *rough masses* even more than any specific combinations. Now, to produce any given body in a *given place* or *situation*, and of *given dimensions* or *shape*, the odds against it are at least *one power of infinite* to obtain the *place* or situation; when the *situation* is obtained, the *solid content* requires another *power of infinite* to obtain it; once the *situation* and *solidity* are obtained, accomplishing even the *simplest given shape* requires at least the other three *powers of infinite*. For instance, let the shape be a *four-sided prism* or *parallelepiped*; that the *surfaces* should be *planes* requires *one power*; that they should be *parallel* in this case, or *inclined* at any *given angle* in any other case, requires *another power of infinite*; and that they should be in any *given ratio* to each other requires at least the *third power*: for in each of these categories there is still at least an *infinity* of other possible cases besides the one *given*. So that all

None but the barest *possibility* against an inconceivably great *probability*, surpassing everything which is not *strict demonstration*.

This infinity, combined with the infinite cases of the previous parts, raises the odds by a power of infinity.

<margin>General causes.</margin>
XIX. THERE is another kind of *beauty* as well which is still pleasing to our *sense*, and from which we conclude there is *wisdom* in the *cause* as well as *design*, and that is when we see many *useful* or *beautiful effects* flowing from *one general cause*. There is a very good *reason* for this conclusion among men. *Interest* must lead beings of limited powers, who are incapable of a great diversity of operations and are distracted by them, to choose this *frugal economy* of their forces, and to look upon such management as evidence of *wisdom* in other beings like themselves. Nor is this *speculative reason* all which influences them, for even besides this consideration of *interest*, they are determined by a *sense of beauty* where that *reason* does not apply; as when we are judging the productions of other *agents* about whose economy we are not concerned. The uniform principle of *Gravity* preserves at once the *Planets* in their orbits, gives *Cohesion* to the parts of each globe, and *Stability* to *Mountains*, *Hills*, and artificial *structures*; it raises the sea in tides, and sinks them again, and restrains them in their channels; it drains the *Earth* of its superfluous moisture by *Rivers*; it raises the *Vapors* by its influence on the *Air*, and brings them down again in *Rains*; it gives a uniform *Pressure* to our *Atmosphere*, necessary to our bodies in general, and more especially to *Inspiration* in breathing; and furnishes us with a universal *Movement*, capable of being applied in innumerable engines. How incomparably more beautiful is this structure than if we supposed so many distinct *Volitions* in the DEITY, producing every particular effect, and preventing some of the accidental evils which casually flow from the *general law!* We may rashly imagine that this latter manner of operation might have been more useful to us, and it would have been no distraction to *Omnipotence*; but then the great *Beauty* would have been lost, and there would have been no more pleasure in the contemplation of this scene, which is now so delightful. # S E C T. VI.

## Of the Universality of the Sense of Beauty among Men.

<margin>
Internal sense not an immediate source of pain.
</margin>

But our *Inquiry* is confined to men; and before we examine the *universality* of this *sense* of *beauty*, or their agreement in approving *uniformity*, it may be appropriate to consider, “whether, just as the other *senses* which give us pleasure also give us pain, so this *sense* of *beauty* does make some objects disagreeable to us, and the occasion of pain.”

THAT many objects give no pleasure to our sense is obvious; many are certainly void of *beauty*: But then there is no form which seems necessarily disagreeable of itself, when we dread no other evil

### Sect. 6.

<margin>
*Approval and Dislike from Associations of Ideas.*

II. **There** are indeed many faces which at first view are likely to raise dislike; but this is generally not from any positive deformity which of itself is positively displeasing, but either from a lack of expected beauty, or much more from their carrying some natural indications of *morally bad dispositions*, which we all acquire a faculty of discerning in *countenances, airs, and gestures*. That this is not caused by any form that is inherently disgusting will appear from this: that if upon long acquaintance we are sure of finding *sweetness of temper, humanity, and cheerfulness*, although the bodily form remains the same, it shall give us no disgust or displeasure; whereas if anything were naturally disagreeable, or the cause of pain or positive distaste, it would always remain so, even although the aversion we might have toward it were counterbalanced by other considerations. There are horrors raised by some objects which are only the effect of fear for ourselves, or *compassion* toward others, when either *reason* or some foolish *association of ideas* makes us apprehend danger, and not the effect of anything in the form itself; for we find that most of those objects which excite horror at first, when experience or reason has removed the fear, may become the occasions of pleasure; as *ravenous*

<page-num>81</page-num>
<header>BEAUTY, ORDER, &c.</header>

### Sect. 6.

<margin>
Diversity of judgments concerning our senses.
</margin>

VIII. It deserves our consideration on this subject how, in similar cases, we form very different judgments concerning the *internal* and *external senses*. Nothing is more common among those who, following Mr. *LOCKE*, have rejected the groundless opinions about *innate ideas*, than to allege, “That all our *relish* for *beauty* and *order* comes either from the prospect of *advantage*, *custom*, or *education*,” for no other reason than the *variety* of *tastes* in the world; and from this they conclude, “That our *tastes* do not arise from any *natural power of perception, or sense*.” And yet everyone allows our *external senses* to be *natural*, and that the pleasures or pains of their sensations—however they may be increased or diminished by *custom* or *education*, and counterbalanced by *interest*—are really prior to *custom, habit, education*, or the *prospect* of *interest*. Now it is certain “that there is at least as great a variety of *tastes* concerning their *objects* as there is concerning the *objects* of *beauty*.” In fact, it is much more difficult, and perhaps impossible, to bring the *tastes* or *relishes* of the *external senses* to any general foundation at all, or to find any *rule* for what is *agreeable* or *disagreeable*; and yet we all allow “that these are *natural powers of perception*.”

<header>An INQUIRY concerning</header>

### Sect. 6.

---

<margin>
The reason for it.
</margin>
IX. The reason for this different judgment can be nothing other than this: that we have distinct names for the *external senses*, and none, or very few, for the *internal*; and by this we are led, as in many other cases, to look upon the former as somehow more *fixed*, *real*, and *natural* than the latter. The *sense of harmony* has its name, namely, a *good ear*; and we are generally led to acknowledge this as a *natural power of perception*, or a *sense* somehow distinct from *hearing*: now it is certain "that there is as necessary a perception of *beauty* upon the presence of *regular objects*, as of *harmony* upon hearing certain *sounds*."

<margin>
An internal sense does not presuppose innate ideas.
</margin>
X. But let it be observed here once and for all, "that an *internal sense* no more presupposes an *innate idea* or *principle* of knowledge than the *external*." Both are natural *powers of perception*, or *determinations* of the mind to necessarily receive certain ideas from the presence of objects. The *internal sense* is a *passive power of receiving ideas of beauty from all objects in which there is uniformity amidst variety*. Nor does there seem to be anything more difficult in this matter than that the mind should be always determined to receive the idea of *sweet* when particles of such a form enter the pores of the

# SECTION VII.

## Of the Power of *Custom*, *Education*, and *Example*, as to our *internal Senses*.

I. CUSTOM, *Education*, and *Example* are so often alleged in this affair as the occasion of our *relish* for *beautiful objects*, and for our *approbation* of, or *delight* in a certain *conduct* in life, in a *moral sense*, that it is necessary to examine these three particularly, to make it appear "that there is a *natural power* of *perception*, or *sense of beauty* in *objects*, antecedent to all *custom*, *edu-cation*, or *example*."

<margin>*Custom gives no new sense;*</margin>
II. CUSTOM, as distinct from the other two, operates in this manner. As to actions, it only gives a disposition to the mind or body more easily to perform those actions which have been frequently repeated, but never leads us to apprehend them under any other view than what we were capable of apprehending them under at first; nor gives us any new power of perception about them. We are naturally capable of sentiments of fear, and dread of any powerful presence; and

<meta>catchword: fo</meta>

<summary>The author begins an investigation into whether our appreciation for beauty and morality is merely a product of custom, education, and example, arguing instead for an innate power of perception. He asserts that custom only makes actions easier to perform rather than creating entirely new sensory perceptions.</summary>
