just as custom may connect the ideas of religious *horror* to certain buildings; but *custom* could never have made a *being* naturally incapable of *fear* receive such ideas. Similarly, if we had no other power of perceiving or forming ideas of actions except as they were *advantageous* or *disadvantageous*, *custom* could only have made us more ready to perceive the *advantage* or *disadvantage* of actions. But this is not relevant to our present purpose.

Regarding our approval of, or delight in, external objects: when the *blood* or *spirits* of which *anatomists* speak are aroused, quickened, or fermented (as they call it) in any agreeable manner by medicine or nourishment, or when any glands are frequently stimulated to secretion, it is certain that to keep the body comfortable, we shall delight in objects of *taste* which are not immediately pleasant in themselves, if they promote that agreeable state to which the body has been *accustomed*. Furthermore, *custom* will so alter the state of the body that what at first raised uneasy sensations will cease to do so, or perhaps raise another agreeable idea within the same sense; but *custom* can never give us any idea of a sense different from those we had prior to it. It will never make the *blind* approve of objects as colored, or those who have no *taste* approve of foods as *delicious*, regardless of how they

Were our *glands* and the parts around them void of feeling, or did we perceive no pleasure from certain brisker motions in the *blood*, *custom* could never make stimulating or intoxicating fluids or medicines agreeable if they were not already so to the taste. So, by similar reasoning, had we no natural sense of *beauty* from *uniformity*, *custom* could never have made us imagine any *beauty* in objects; if we had no *ear*, *custom* could never have given us the pleasures of *harmony*. When we have these natural senses beforehand, *custom* may make us capable of extending our views further and of receiving more complex ideas of *beauty* in bodies, or *harmony* in sounds, by increasing our attention and quickness of perception. But however *custom* may increase our power of receiving or comparing complex ideas, yet it seems rather to weaken than strengthen the ideas of *beauty*, or the impressions of pleasure from regular objects; otherwise, how is it possible that any person could go into the open air on a sunny day or clear evening without the most extravagant raptures, such as MILTON <note>See *Paradise Lost*, Book 8.</note> represents our *ancestor* <note>Adam</note> in upon his first creation? For such raptures any person would certainly fall into upon the first representation of such a scene.

In the same way, custom may make it easier for any person to perceive the use of a complex machine and approve of it as advantageous; but he would never have imagined it *beautiful* if he had no *natural sense* of *beauty*. *Custom* may make us quicker in understanding the truth of complex *theorems*, but we all find the pleasure or *beauty* of theorems to be as strong at first as it ever is. *Custom* makes us more capable of remembering and comparing complex ideas, so as to perceive more complicated *uniformity*, which escapes the observation of *novices* in any art; but all this assumes a *natural sense* of *beauty* in *uniformity*. For if there were nothing in forms that naturally caused pleasure to our senses, then no repetition of ideas that are indifferent to pleasure or pain, *beauty* or *deformity*, could ever have made them become pleasing or displeasing.

<margin>Nor Education.</margin>
III. THE effect of EDUCATION is this: through it, we receive many speculative opinions, which are sometimes true and sometimes false; and we are often led to believe that objects may be naturally inclined to give pleasure or pain to our external senses, even though in reality they have no such qualities. Furthermore, through *education*, there are some strong associations of ideas formed without any reason—sometimes by mere accident, as well as

We know what sickness of the stomach is and may believe without grounds that very healthful meats will cause this; through our sight and smell, we receive disagreeable ideas of the food of swine and their sties, and perhaps cannot prevent these ideas from recurring at the table: but men who are naturally *blind* were never prejudiced against objects as being of a disagreeable color, or in favor of others as being of a beautiful color; they perhaps hear men dispraise one color and may imagine this color to be some quite different sensible quality of the other senses, but that is all: and in the same way, a man naturally void of *taste* could by no *education* receive the ideas of taste or be prejudiced in favor of meats as delicious. And then, if the finest objects are presented to us, we become conscious of a pleasure far superior to what common performances excite. But all this presupposes our *sense of beauty* to be *natural*. Instruction in *anatomy*, observation of *nature*, and of those *expressions* of the countenance and *attitudes* of body which accompany any *sentiment*, *action*, or *passion*, may enable us to know where there is a just *imitation*; but why should an exact *imitation* please upon observation if we did not naturally have a *sense of beauty* in it, any more than observing the placement of fifty or a hundred pebbles thrown at random? And should we observe them ever so often, we would never dream of them becoming *beautiful*.

Prejudices, how removed.</margin>
IV. THERE is something worth our observation regarding the manner of rooting out the *prejudices of education*, which is not at all foreign to the present purpose. When a *prejudice* arises from *associations of ideas* that have no natural connection, we must frequently force ourselves to endure representations of those objects, or the use of them when they are separated from the disagreeable idea; and this may at last break the unreasonable association, especially if we can join new, agreeable ideas to them. Thus, opinions of *superstition* are best removed by pleasant conversation with persons we esteem for their *virtue*, or by observing that they despise such opinions. But when a *prejudice* arises from an apprehension or opinion of *natural evil* as the accompaniment or consequence of any object or action; if the *evil* is understood to be the constant and immediate accompaniment, a few trials without receiving any damage will remove the prejudice, as in the case of prejudices against certain *meats* <note>In this context, "meats" refers to food in general.</note>. But where the *evil* is not represented as a perpetual accompaniment, but as something that may possibly or probably accompany the use of the object at some time or another, there must be frequent reasoning with ourselves, or a long series of trials without any detriment, to remove the *prejudice*; such is the case with our fear of *spirits* in the dark and in churchyards. *Example* may make us conclude without examination that our countrymen have obtained the perfection of *beauty* in their *works*, or that there is less *beauty* in the orders of *architecture* or *painting* used in other *nations*, and so content ourselves with very imperfect *forms*. And fear of contempt for being void of *taste* or *genius* often makes us join in approving the performances of the reputed masters in our country, and restrains those who have *naturally* a fine *genius*, or very acute internal *senses*, from studying to obtain the greatest perfection; it also makes those of a bad *taste* pretend to a perception of *beauty* which in reality they have not. But all this presupposes some natural power of receiving ideas of *beauty* and *harmony*. Nor can *example* effect anything further, unless it be to lead men to pursue objects by implicit faith, for some perfection which the pursuer is conscious he does not know, or which perhaps is some very different quality from the idea perceived by those of a good *taste* in such *affairs*.

# SECT. VIII.

## *Of the Importance of the internal Senses in Life, and the final Causes of them.*

<margin>Importance of the internal senses.</margin>
I. THE busy part of mankind may look upon these things as airy dreams of an inflamed imagination, which a wise man should despise—one who rationally pursues more solid possessions independent of fancy. But a little reflection will convince us, “That the gratifications of our *internal senses* are as natural, real, and *satisfying enjoyments* as any sensible pleasure whatsoever; and that they are the chief ends for which we commonly pursue *wealth and power*.” For how is *wealth or power advantageous*? How do they make us *happy*, or prove *good* to us? No otherwise than as they supply gratifications to our *senses* or *faculties* of perceiving pleasure. Now, are these *senses* or *faculties* only the *external* ones? No: everyone sees that a small portion of *wealth or power* will supply more pleasures of the *external senses* than we can enjoy; we know that scarcity often heightens these perceptions more than a-

And there are some confused *imaginations* which often lead us to pursue *property*, even in objects where it is not necessary for the true enjoyment of them. These are the ultimate motives for our pursuit of greater degrees of *wealth* when there are no generous intentions for virtuous actions.

THIS is confirmed by the constant practice of the very enemies of these *senses*. As soon as they think they have risen above the *world*, or extricated themselves from the rushes of *avarice* and *ambition*, banished *nature* will return to them and set them upon pursuits of *beauty* and *order* in their *houses*, *gardens*, *dress*, *table*, and *equipage* <note>A carriage and horses with attendants.</note>. They are never at ease without some degree of this; and if their hearts were open to our view, we would see *regularity*, *decency*, and *beauty* as the goals of their wishes, either for themselves or their posterity, and as what their imagination is always presenting to them as the possible effects of their labors. Without this, they could never justify their pursuits to themselves.

THERE may perhaps be some instances of human nature perverted into a thorough *miser* who loves nothing but money, and whose imagination rises no higher than the cold

If we examine the pursuits of the *luxurious*, who in the opinion of the world is wholly devoted to his belly; we shall generally find that the far greater part of his expense is employed to procure sensations other than those of taste; such as *fine attendants*, *regular apartments*, *services of plate* <note>Expensive gold or silver tableware.</note>, and the like. Besides, a large share of the preparation must be supposed to be designed for some sort of generous, friendly purposes, as to please *acquaintances*, *strangers*, and *parasites*. How few would be contented to enjoy the same sensations alone, in a cottage, or from earthenware pitchers? To conclude this point, however these *internal sensations* may be overlooked in our philosophical inquiries about human faculties, we shall find in fact, "That they employ us more, and are more efficacious in *life*, leading either to our *pleasure* or *uneasiness*, than all our *external senses* taken together."

BUT to return to the questions: What occurs to resolve them may be contained in the following propositions.

1. THE manner of knowledge by *universal theorems*, and of operation by *universal causes*, as far as we can attain it, must be most convenient for *beings* of limited understanding and power; since this prevents *distraction* in their understandings through the multiplicity of propositions, and prevents toil and weariness in their powers of action. Consequently, their *reason*, even without any *sense of beauty*, must approve of such methods when they reflect upon their apparent *advantage*.

2. THOSE objects of contemplation in which there is *uniformity amidst variety* are more distinctly and easily comprehended and retained than *irregular objects*; because the accurate observation of one or two parts often leads to the knowledge of the whole. Thus, from a *pillar* or two, with an intermediate *arch* and *cornice*, we can form a distinct idea of a whole regular *building*, if we know of what species it is and have its length and

These principles suggest why a sense of beauty based on order is beneficial to human reason.</summary>

<image-desc>Double horizontal wavy line ornament</image-desc>

Breadth: From a *side* and *solid angle*, we have the whole *regular solid*; measuring one *side* gives the whole *square*; one *radius* gives the whole *circle*; two *diameters* an *oval*; one *ordinate* and *abscissa* the *parabola*; and so on in more complex figures which have any *regularity*, which can be entirely determined and known in every part from a few *data*. Whereas it must require long attention to a vast multiplicity of parts to ascertain or fix the *idea* of any *irregular form*, or to give any distinct idea of it, or to make us capable of retaining it; as appears in the forms of *rough rocks*, *pebbles*, and *confused heaps*, even when the multitude of perceptible parts is not as great as in the *regular forms*. For such *irregular objects* distract the mind with *variety*, since for every perceptible part we must have a completely different idea.

And possibly, the DEITY could have formed us so as to have received no pleasure from such objects, or could have connected pleasure to those of a completely opposite nature. We have a reasonable indication of this in the *beauties* of various animals; they give some small pleasure, indeed, to everyone who views them, but then everyone seems vastly more delighted with the unique *beauties* of their own *species* than with those of a different one, which seldom raise any desire except among animals of the same species as the one being admired. This makes it probable that the *pleasure* is not the necessary *result* of the *form* itself—otherwise it would equally affect all perceptions in whatever *species*—but depends upon a voluntary constitution, adapted to preserve the *regularity* of the *universe*, and is probably not the *effect* of *necessity*

### AN
# INQUIRY
### CONCERNING
## MORAL GOOD and EVIL.

---

## INTRODUCTION.

<margin>Moral Good and Evil.</margin>
THE Word MORAL GOODNESS, in this treatise, denotes our idea of *some quality apprehended in actions, which procures approbation, and love toward the actor, from those who receive no advantage by the action.* MORAL EVIL denotes our idea of *a contrary quality, which excites aversion, and dislike toward the actor, even from persons unconcerned in its natural tendency.* We must be contented with these imperfect descriptions, until we discover whether we really have such ideas, and what general foundation there is in nature for this difference.

<vocab>Moral Goodness, Moral Evil, Quality, Approbation, Actor, Aversion, Nature</vocab>

<summary>The author defines moral goodness and evil as qualities in actions that inspire approval or dislike in observers who do not personally benefit or suffer from the action. These definitions serve as the starting point for exploring whether such moral ideas have a foundation in human nature.</summary>
<keywords>Moral Goodness, Moral Evil, Approbation, Aversion, Ethics, Human Nature, Moral Philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 146 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction to the inquiry concerning moral good and evil, specifically contrasting moral qualities with natural advantages or misfortunes.</meta>

# An INQUIRY concerning the Difference of Actions, as morally Good or Evil.

THESE descriptions seem to contain a universally acknowledged difference between *moral* good and *evil*, and *natural* good and evil. All men who speak of *moral good* acknowledge that it procures *love* toward those we perceive to be possessed of it; whereas *natural good* does not. In this matter, men must consult their own hearts. How differently are they affected toward those they suppose possessed of *honesty, faith, generosity, and kindness*, even when they expect no benefit from these admired *qualities*; and those who are possessed of *natural goods*, such as *houses, lands, gardens, vineyards, health, strength, and sagacity*? We shall find that we necessarily love and approve the possessors of the former; but the possession of the latter procures no *love* at all toward the possessor, but often the contrary feelings of *envy* and *hatred*. 

In the same manner, whatever quality we perceive to be *morally evil* raises our *hatred* toward the person in whom we observe it—such as *treachery, cruelty, and ingratitude*—even when they are in no way harmful to ourselves; whereas we heartily love, esteem, and pity many who are exposed to *natural evils*, such as *pain, poverty, hunger, sickness, and death*, even when we

<summary>The author distinguishes between moral and natural goods, noting that moral virtues inspire love and approval regardless of personal benefit, while natural advantages often inspire envy. Similarly, moral evils provoke hatred even if they do not harm the observer, whereas natural evils like poverty or illness tend to provoke pity.</summary>
<keywords>moral good, natural good, moral evil, natural evil, virtue, vice, love, hatred, pity, envy, human nature</keywords>

--- Page 147 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of how natural evils affect us and begins to define the terminology of "interest," "advantage," and "natural good" as they relate to human pleasure and self-love.</meta>

->MORAL GOOD and EVIL.<-
I
<meta>catchword: or</meta>

we ourselves suffer inconveniences from these natural evils of others.

Now the first question on this subject is, “Where do these different ideas of actions come from?”

<margin>Interest. Advantage.</margin>
BECAUSE we will frequently use the words *interest*, *advantage*, and *natural good* later on, it is necessary to define their meanings here. The pleasure in our sensory perceptions of any kind gives us our first idea of *natural good* or *happiness*; consequently, all objects that are likely to excite this pleasure are called *immediately good*. Those objects which may obtain others that are immediately pleasant are called *advantageous*; we pursue both kinds from a perspective of *interest* or *self-love*.

OUR sense of pleasure comes before *advantage* or *interest* and is the foundation of them. We do not perceive pleasure in objects because it is in our *interest* to do so; rather, objects or actions are *advantageous*—and are pursued or undertaken out of *interest*—because we receive pleasure from them. Our perception of pleasure is necessary, and nothing is *advantageous* or *naturally good* to us unless it is likely to produce pleasure either mediately <note>Indirectly, or through an intermediate step.</note> or immediately. Such objects as we know, either from sensory experience or reason, to be immediately,

<summary>The author defines natural good and happiness as rooted in sensory pleasure, distinguishing between things that are immediately good and those that are advantageous. He argues that pleasure is the foundation of interest and self-love, rather than interest being the cause of pleasure.</summary>
<keywords>natural good, happiness, interest, advantage, self-love, pleasure, sensory perception, moral philosophy</keywords>

or indirectly *advantageous*, or likely to provide *pleasure*, we are said to pursue from *self-interest* when our intention is only to enjoy this *pleasure* which they have the power of exciting. Thus *meats*, *drink*, *harmony*, fine *prospects*, *painting*, *statues*, are perceived by our senses to be *immediately* good; and our reason shows *riches* and *power* to be indirectly so—that is, likely to furnish us with objects of immediate *pleasure*. Both kinds of these *natural* goods are pursued from *interest* or *self-love*.

<margin>Opinions about our sense of moral good and evil.</margin>
Now the greatest part of our recent *moralists* establish it as undeniable, "That all *moral qualities* necessarily have some relation to the *law* of a *superior* of sufficient power to make us *happy* or *miserable*;" and since all *laws* operate only by *sanctions* of *rewards* or *punishments*, which lead us to obedience by motives of *self-interest*, they suppose, "that it is in this way that *laws* make some actions *indirectly* good, or *advantageous*, and others in the same way *disadvantageous*." They say indeed, "That a benevolent *legislator* makes no actions *advantageous* to the agent by law except those which in their own nature tend to the *natural* good of the *whole*, or, at least, are not inconsistent with it; and that therefore we approve

II. ## S E C T. I.

### *Of the Moral Sense by which we perceive Virtue and Vice, and approve or disapprove them in others.*

<margin>Different Ideas of Moral and Natural Good.</margin>
I. THAT the perceptions of *moral good and evil* are perfectly different from those of *natural good*, or *advantage*, everyone must convince himself by reflecting on the different manner in which he finds himself affected when these objects occur to him. If we had no *sense* of *good* distinct from the *advantage* or *interest* arising from the external senses and the perceptions of *beauty* and *harmony*, our admiration and love toward a fruitful field or convenient habitation would be much the same as what we have toward a generous *friend* or any noble *character*; for both are, or may be, advantageous to us. And we should no more admire any action or love any person in a distant country or age whose influence could not extend to us than we love the mountains of PERU while we are unconcerned with the *Spanish* trade. We should have the same sentiments and affections toward

Sect. 1. <image-desc>decorative typographic flourish underline</image-desc> Cruelty would be as calmly reacted to as a *blight*, or *mildew*, or an overflowing stream. But I imagine everyone is affected very differently on these occasions, even though there may be an equal amount of *natural evil* in both. In fact, actions that are in no way harmful may cause the strongest anger and indignation if they show only powerless hatred or contempt. And, on the other hand, the introduction of *moral ideas* may prevent us from hating the person, or having a negative moral view of the action, which causes us the greatest *natural evil*. Thus, the belief that there is *justice* in any legal sentence will prevent all ideas of *moral evil* in its execution, or any hatred toward the *magistrate*, who is the immediate cause of our greatest sufferings.

<margin>
*In actions toward others.*
</margin>
II. In our feelings regarding actions which affect ourselves, there is indeed a mixture of the ideas of *natural* and *moral good*, which requires some attention to separate. But when we reflect upon actions which affect other persons only, we may observe *moral ideas* unmixed with those of *natural good* or *evil*. For let it be noted here that those *senses* by which we perceive pleasure in *natural objects*—from which they are considered *advantageous*—could never raise in us any desire for the *public good*, but only for what was good for ourselves.

<summary>The author argues that our reaction to cruelty differs from our reaction to natural disasters because of a moral sense, and that justice can mitigate our hatred of those who cause us natural harm. He also notes that we can best observe pure moral ideas when judging actions that affect others rather than ourselves.</summary>

III. SOME refined explainers of *self-love* may tell us, “That we hate or love *characters* based on whether we believe we would have been supported or injured by them, had we lived in their *days*.” But how obvious is the answer if we only observe that if we had no sense of moral *good* in *humanity*, *mercy*, and *faithfulness*, why wouldn't *self-love* and our sense of natural *good* always engage us on the victorious side and make us admire and love the successful *tyrant* or *traitor*? Why do we not love *SINON* or *PYRRHUS* in the *Aeneid*? For had we been *GREEKS*, these two would have been very advantageous *characters*. Why are we affected by the fortunes of *PRIAMUS*, *POLITES*, *CHOROEBUS*, or *AENEAS*? It is plain that we have some secret sense which determines our *approbation* without regard to *self-interest*; otherwise, we would always favor the fortunate side without regard to *virtue* and suppose ourselves to be engaged with that party.

**Should** any of our travelers find some old *Grecian treasure*, the *miser* who hid it certainly performed an action more to the traveler’s *advantage* than **Codrus** or **Orestes** did; for he must have but a small share of benefit from their actions, whose influence is so dispersed and lost across various ages and nations. Surely then, this *miser* must appear to the traveler as a prodigious hero in *virtue*! **IF** we had no concept of actions besides our opinion of their *advantage* or *disadvantage*, could we ever choose an action as *advantageous* while being conscious that it is still *evil*? Yet this happens all too often in human affairs. Why would there be a need for such *high bribes* to persuade men to abandon the interests of a ruined party, or for tortures to force out the secrets of their friends? Is it so difficult to convince men's understandings—if that were the only faculty we possessed—that it is likely more advantageous to secure present gain and avoid present evils by joining the winning side, rather than waiting for the remote possibility of future good from a revolution that is often improbable and sometimes unexpected? And when men are over-persuaded by *advantage*, do they always approve of their own conduct? In fact, how often does the rest of their life feel odious and shameful in their own eyes, as well as in the eyes of others, even those for whom the base action was profitable?

### Sect. I.

If anyone becomes satisfied with his own conduct in such a case, on what ground is it? How does he please himself, or justify his actions to others? Never by reflecting upon his *private advantage*, or claiming this to others as a justification; but by gradually shifting into the *moral principles* of his *new party*; for no party is without them. And thus men become pleased with their actions under some appearance of *moral good*, distinct from *advantage*.

<header>MORAL GOOD and EVIL. Sect. I.</header>

But further, though these *rewards* and *punishments* may make my own *actions* appear *advantageous* to me and make me approve them from *self-love*, yet they would never make me approve and love another *person* for similar *actions* whose *merit* would not be imputed to me. Those *actions* are *advantageous* indeed to the *agent*; but his *advantage* is not my *advantage*: and *self-love* could never influence me to approve *actions* as *advantageous* to others, or to love the *authors* of them on that account.

---

<margin>*Our moral sense of the actions of others cannot be bribed.*</margin>
This is the second thing to be considered: "Whether our *sense* of *moral* *good* or *evil* in the *actions* of others can be overbalanced or bribed by *views* of *interest*." Now **I** may indeed easily be capable of wishing that another would do an *action* I abhor as *morally evil* if it were very *advantageous* to me; *interest* in that case may overbalance my *desire* for *virtue* in another. But no *interest* to *myself* will make me approve an *action* as morally *good* which, without that *interest* to *myself*, would have appeared morally *evil*; if, upon computing its whole *effects*, it appears to produce as great a moment of *good* in the *whole* when it is not beneficial to me as it did before when it was. AGAIN, according to this scheme, what could a statue or *panegyric* accomplish? Men love *praise*. They will perform the actions that they observe to be *praised*. *Praise*, to men who have no other idea of good but *self-interest*, is the opinion that a *nation* or *party* has of a man as being *useful* to them. REGULUS, or CATO, or DECIUS, received no advantage from the actions that profited their country, and therefore they themselves could not admire those actions, however much the persons who reaped the advantage might praise them. REGULUS or CATO could not possibly praise or love another hero for a *virtuous action*; for this would not gain them the *advantage* of *honor*. They must have looked upon their own actions as the hard terms on which *honor* was to be purchased, without there being anything amiable in them that they could contemplate or reflect upon with *pleasure*. Now, how unlike this is to what the slightest observation would teach a man concerning such characters!

<page-num>133</page-num>
<header>MORAL GOOD and EVIL.</header>

“Actions bring *honor* to him only, Sect. 1.
“without any *advantage* to ourselves?”

It is true that the actions we approve of are useful to mankind, but not always to the person who approves of them. It would perhaps be useful to the *whole* of society if all men agreed to perform such actions, and then everyone would have his share of the *advantage*. But this only proves that *reason* and calm *reflection* may recommend to us, out of *self-interest*, those actions which, at first view, our *moral sense* leads us to admire without considering this *interest*. Indeed, our *sense* shall operate even where the *advantage* to ourselves does not apply. We can approve of the *justice* of a sentence against ourselves: a condemned *traitor* may approve of the vigilance of a CICERO in discovering conspiracies, even though it would have been for the *traitor’s advantage* if there had never been any men of such *sagacity* in the world. To say that he may still approve of such conduct as tending toward the *public good* is a joke, coming from someone whose only idea of good is *self-interest*. Such a person has no desire for the *public good* further than it tends toward his own *advantage*, which it does not do at all in the present case.

<margin>Nor custom, education, etc.</margin>
VII. IF what has been said makes it appear that we have some other *amiable idea* of actions than that of being *advantageous* to ourselves, we may conclude, “That this

<summary>The author argues that our moral sense operates independently of self-interest, as evidenced by our ability to admire justice and sagacity even when they result in our own disadvantage. He concludes that approval of the public good cannot be explained by selfish motives alone.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the argument that moral ideas are not derived from self-interest. Having previously argued that we can approve of justice even when it harms us, the author now asserts that moral perception is an innate sense rather than a product of education or custom.</meta>

<page-num>134</page-num>
<header>An INQUIRY concerning</header>

<margin>Sect. I.</margin>

> “The perception of *moral* good is not derived from *custom*, *education*, *example*, or *study*.”

These things give us no new ideas. They might make us see an *advantage* to ourselves in actions whose usefulness did not at first appear, or give us opinions of some tendency of actions to our *detriment* by some subtle deductions of *reason* or by a rash prejudice, even when we would have observed no such thing upon first viewing the action. But they never could have made us perceive actions as *amiable* or *odious* without any consideration of our own *advantage*.

VIII. It remains then, 
> “That as the AUTHOR of *Nature* has determined us to receive, through our external *senses*, pleasant or disagreeable ideas of objects according to whether they are useful or hurtful to our bodies; and to receive from uniform objects the pleasures of *beauty* and *harmony* to excite us to the pursuit of knowledge and to reward us for it—or to be an argument to us of his *goodness*, as the *uniformity* itself proves his *existence*, whether we had a *sense* of *beauty* in *uniformity* or not—in the same manner, he has given us a MORAL SENSE to direct our actions and to give us still nobler pleasures; so that while we are only intending the *good* of others, we...”

<meta>catchword: unde-</meta>

<summary>The author argues that moral perception is an innate sense provided by the Author of Nature, rather than a learned behavior or a calculation of self-interest. Just as we have physical senses and a sense of beauty, we possess a moral sense that allows us to find actions amiable or odious independently of their benefit to ourselves.</summary>

WE are not to imagine that this moral *sense*, any more than the other *senses*, supposes any *innate ideas*, *knowledge*, or *practical propositions*. We mean by it only a *determination* of our minds to receive *amiable* or *disagreeable ideas* of *actions* when they occur to our *observation*, prior to any *opinions* of *advantage* or *loss* to ourselves resulting from them; just as we are pleased with a *regular form* or an *harmonious composition* without having any *knowledge* of *mathematics* or seeing any *advantage* in that *form* or *composition* other than the immediate *pleasure*.

## Section II.

### Concerning the immediate Motive to virtuous Actions.

The *motives* of human *actions*, or their immediate *causes*, would be best understood after considering the *passions* and *affections*; but here we shall only consider the *springs* of the actions which we call *virtuous*, as far as it is necessary to settle the general foundation of the *moral sense*.

<margin>
Affections, the motives to actions.
</margin>

I. Every action, which we perceive as either *morally good* or *evil*, is always supposed to flow from some *affection* toward rational *agents*; and whatever we call *virtue* or *vice*, is either some such *affection*, or some *action* consequent upon it. Or it may perhaps be enough to make an action, or omission, appear *vicious*, if it indicates the lack of such *affection* toward rational agents, as we expect in characters considered *morally good*. All the actions considered *religious* in any country, are supposed, by those who consider them so, to flow from some affections toward the Deity; and whatever we call *social virtue*, we still suppose to flow from

<summary>This section begins an investigation into the motives behind human behavior, arguing that moral virtue and vice are rooted in the "affections" one feels toward rational beings or the Deity. It suggests that an action is judged as good or evil based on whether it demonstrates the presence or absence of these benevolent internal drives.</summary>

<margin>Disinterested affections.</margin>
II. <margin>Love of satisfaction and hatred of displeasure.</margin>
The affections that are of the most importance in *morals* are LOVE and HATRED; all the rest seem to be but different modifications of these two *original affections*. Now, in discussing *love* toward rational agents, we do not need to be cautioned not to include that *love* between the *sexes* which, when no other *affections* accompany it, is only a *desire for pleasure* and is never counted as a *virtue*. *Love* toward rational agents is subdivided into <term>Love of Complacence or Esteem</term> and <term>Love of Benevolence</term>.

<margin>
Self-love joined with benevolence

BUT it must be observed here, that as all men have *self-love* as well as *benevolence*, these two principles may jointly move a man to the same *action*; and then they are to be considered as two forces driving the same body into motion; sometimes they work together, sometimes they are independent of each other, and sometimes they are in some degree opposite. Thus, if a man has such strong *benevolence* that it would have produced an *action* without any views of *self-interest*, the fact that such a man also has in view *private advantage*, along with the *public good*, as the effect of his *action*, in no way diminishes the *benevolence* of the *action*. When he would not have produced as much *public good* if it were not for the prospect of *self-interest*, then the effect of *self-love* is to be subtracted, and his *benevolence* is proportional to the remainder of the *good* which pure *benevolence* would have produced. When a man’s *benevolence* is harmful to himself, then *self-love* is opposite to *benevolence*, and the *benevolence* is proportional to the sum of the *good* produced, added to the resistance of *self-love* overcome by it. In most cases, it is impossible for men to know how far their fellows are influenced by one or the other of these principles; but yet the

# An INQUIRY concerning

Sect. 2.
<image-desc>A small decorative horizontal scrollwork line.</image-desc>

“*love*;” such as *fear* or *reverence*, arising from a *perception* of *goodness*, *power*, and *justice*. For no one perceives any *virtue* in *base dread* and *servitude* toward a powerful *evil being*: this is indeed the lowest *selfishness*. Now, the same arguments which prove *love of esteem* to be *disinterested* will prove this honorable *reverence* to be so too; for it plainly arises from a perception of *amiable qualities* in the person, and *love* toward him, which raises an *abhorrence* of offending him. Could we reverence a *being* because it was our *interest* to do so, a third person might bribe us into reverence toward a *being* neither *good* nor *powerful*, which everyone sees to be a joke. And we might show this to be common to all other passions that have rational agents for their objects.

means of obtaining their *bounty*? If it were so, then we could indifferently love any *character*, even to obtain the *bounty* of a third *person*; or we could be bribed by a third *person* to love the greatest *villain* heartily, just as we may be bribed to perform external *offices*: now this is plainly impossible.

But further, is not our *love* always the *result* of *bounty*, and not the means of obtaining it? External show, obsequiousness, and dissimulation may precede an *opinion* of *beneficence*; but real *love* always presupposes it, and will necessarily arise even when we expect no more, from the consideration of past benefits. Or can anyone say he only loves the *beneficent* person, as he does a *field* or *garden*, because of its advantage? His *love* then must cease toward one who has ruined himself in kind *offices* to him, when he can do him no more; as we cease to love an inanimate object which ceases to be useful, unless a poetic *prosopopoeia* <note>A rhetorical figure in which an inanimate object or abstract concept is represented as a person.</note> animates it, and raises an imaginary gratitude, which is indeed quite common. And then again, our *love* would be the same toward the worst *characters* as it is toward the best, if they were equally bountiful to us, which is also false. *Beneficence* then must raise our *love* as it is an amiable moral *quality*: and hence we love even those who are beneficent to others.

He notes that we love beneficent people even when they can no longer help us or when they help others, proving our affection is disinterested.</summary>

--- Page 182 ---
<meta>This page continues the inquiry into whether our love for a benefactor is purely self-interested, specifically addressing why we feel more love when we are the direct beneficiaries of bounty.</meta>

<header>An INQUIRY concerning</header>

<margin>
Sect. 2.
~
</margin>

It may be further alleged, "That *bounty* toward ourselves is a stronger incitement to *love* than *equal bounty* toward others." This is true for a reason to be offered below <note>See Section 5, Article 2.</note>; but it does not prove that in this case our *love* of persons is from views of *interest*; since this *love* is not prior to the *bounty*, as the means to procure it, but *subsequent* to it, even when we expect no more. In the benefits which we receive ourselves, we are more fully aware of their value and of the circumstances of the action, which are evidences of a generous temper in the *donor*; and from the good opinion we have of ourselves, we are apt to look upon the *kindness* as better employed than when it is bestowed on others, for whom perhaps we have less favorable sentiments. It is however sufficient to remove the objection to note that *bounty* from a *donor* perceived as *morally evil*, or extorted by *force*, or conferred with some view of *self-interest*, will not procure *real love*; nay, it may raise *indignation*, if we suspect *dissimulation* of *love*, or a design to allure us into anything *dishonorable*: whereas wisely employed *bounty* is always *approved*, and gains love for the *author* from all who hear of it.

<note>See Section 5, Article 2.</note>

<summary>The author explains that while we feel more love for those who are kind to us personally, this is due to a better appreciation of their character and our own self-worth, rather than a selfish desire for gain. He concludes that bounty only inspires true love when it is perceived as a result of genuine moral goodness rather than force or self-interest.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the motives behind virtuous actions, specifically questioning whether our love for others is driven by self-interest or a truly disinterested affection.</meta>

<header>MORAL GOOD and EVIL. Sect. 2.</header>

<margin>
<image-desc>A decorative bracket-like flourish</image-desc>
*Virtue is disinterested.*
</margin>
If, then, no *love* toward *persons* is influenced by *self-love* or *views* of *interest*, and all *virtue* flows from *love* toward *persons* or some other *affection* that is equally *disinterested*, it follows that "there must be some other *motive* than *self-love* or *interest* which drives us to the *actions* we call *virtuous*."

<margin>
*Objection from religion.*
</margin>
VII. There may perhaps still remain another *suspicion* of *self-interest* in our *pursuit* of *virtue*, arising from this: "That the whole *human race* seems persuaded of the *existence* of an *Almighty Being* who will certainly secure *happiness*, either now or *hereafter*, to those who are *virtuous*, according to their various *notions* of *virtue* in different *places*; and based on this *persuasion*, *virtue* may in all *cases* be pursued from *views* <note>See Section 1, Article 5, Paragraph 4 above.</note> of *interest*." Here again, we might appeal to all *mankind*: is there no *benevolence* except what flows from a *view* of *reward* from the DEITY? Nay, do we not see a great deal of it among those who have few, if any, *thoughts* of *devotion* at all? Not to mention that this *benevolence* scarcely deserves the *name* when we neither desire nor delight in the *good* of others any further than it serves our own *ends*.

But if we have no other idea of *good* than *advantage* to ourselves, we must imagine that every rational *being* acts only for its own *advantage*. And however we may call a *beneficent being* a *good being* because it acts for our *advantage*, yet under this *scheme* we would not be likely to think there is any *beneficent being* in *nature*, or a *being* who acts for the *good* of others. Particularly, if there is no *sense of excellence* in *public love* and promoting the *happiness* of others, from where should this persuasion arise: "That the DEITY will make the *virtuous* happy?" Can we prove that it is for the *advantage* of the DEITY to do so? This, I imagine, will be looked upon as very absurd, unless we suppose some beneficent *dispositions* are essential to the DEITY, which lead him to consult the *public good* of his *creatures* and reward those who cooperate with his *kind intention*. And if there are such *dispositions* in the DEITY, where is the impossibility of some small degree of this *public love* in his *creatures*? And why must they be supposed incapable of acting except from *self-love*?

In short, without acknowledging some other *principle of action* in rational *agents* than *self-love*, I see no foundation to expect *beneficence* or *rewards*

What should engage the DEITY to reward *virtue*? *Virtue* is commonly supposed, according to *this scheme*, to be only consulting our own *happiness* in the *most artful way*, *consistently* with the *good* of the *whole*; and in *vice* the same thing is foolishly pursued, in a manner which will not so probably succeed, and which is contrary to the *good* of the *whole*. But how is the DEITY concerned in this *whole*, if every agent always acts from *self-love*? And what ground have we, from the idea of a *God itself*, to believe the DEITY is *good* in the *Christian sense*, that is, *studious of the good of his creatures*? Perhaps the *misery* of his creatures may give him as much pleasure as their *happiness*: And who can find fault with, or blame such a *being* for studying their *misery*; for what else should we expect? A *Manichean evil god* is a notion which men would as readily run into as that of a *good one*, if there is no excellence in *disinterested love*, and no *being* acts but for its *own advantage*; unless we proved that the happiness of *creatures* was *advantageous* to the DEITY.

From concomitant pleasure.

VIII. THE last, and only remaining objection against what has been said, is this: "That *virtue* perhaps is pursued because of the *concomitant pleasure*." To which we may answer, first, by observing that this plainly supposes a *sense* of *virtue* existing before ideas of *advantage*, upon which this *advantage* is founded; and that from the very *frame* of our *nature*, we are determined to perceive *pleasure* in the practice of *virtue*, and to approve it when practiced by ourselves or others.

BUT further, may we not justly question whether all *virtue* is *pleasant*? Or whether we are not determined toward some *amiable actions* in which we find no *pleasure*? It is true that all the *passions* and *affections* justify themselves; or, we approve of our being affected in a certain manner on certain occasions, and condemn a person who is affected differently. So the *sorrowful*, the *angry*, the *jealous*, and the *compassionate* think it reasonable they should be so upon the various occasions which move these *passions*; but we should not therefore say that *sorrow*, *anger*, *jealousy*, or *pity* are pleasant, and that we choose to be in these passions because of the concomitant *pleasure*. The matter is plainly this: The *frame* of our *nature*, on such occasions as move these *passions*, determines us to be thus

<meta>This page continues the argument that certain passions, though painful, are not pursued for private benefit or simply to end personal distress.</meta>

affected, and to approve of our being so: <margin>Sect. 2.</margin>
Indeed, we dislike any person who is not thus affected on such occasions, despite the uneasiness of these passions.
This uneasiness drives us to endeavor to change the state of the object, but not otherwise to remove the painful affection while the occasion remains unchanged. This shows that these affections are neither chosen for their accompanying pleasure, nor voluntarily brought upon ourselves with a view toward *private good*. The actions to which these passions move us generally tend to remove the uneasy passion by altering the state of the object; but the removal of our own pain is seldom directly intended in the uneasy *benevolent passions*. Nor is the change intended in the state of the objects by such passions imagined to be a *private good* to the agent, as it always is in the *selfish passions*. If our sole intention in *compassion* or *pity* was the removal of our own pain, we would run away, shut our eyes, and divert our thoughts from the miserable object to avoid the pain of compassion—which we seldom do. On the contrary, we crowd around such objects and voluntarily expose ourselves to pain, unless reason and reflection on our inability to relieve the miserable overrule our inclination, or some selfish affection, such as fear of danger, overbalances it.

These actions are often accompanied by no pleasure at the time, nor do they have any subsequent pleasure, except insofar as they are successful; unless it is that which may arise from *calm reflection* when the passion is over, upon our having been in a disposition which appears lovely and good to our *moral sense*: but this pleasure is never intended in the heat of action, nor is it any motive exciting us to it.

BESIDES, in the *pleasant passions*, we do not *love* because it is *pleasant* to love; we do not *choose* this state because it is an *advantageous* or *pleasant* state: this passion necessarily arises from seeing its proper *object*, a *morally good character*. And if we could love whenever we see it would be in our *interest* to love, love could be bribed by a third person; and we could never love persons in distress, for then our love gives us pain. The same observation may be extended to all the other affections from which *virtue* is supposed to flow: and from the whole we may conclude, "That the *virtuous agent*

### Sect. 2.

<margin>The true source of virtue.</margin>
IX. HAVING removed these false sources of virtuous actions, let us next establish the true one, namely, some *determination* of our nature to study the *good* of others; or some *instinct*, prior to all *reasoning* from *interest*, which influences us to the *love* of others; just as the *moral sense*,* explained above, determines us to approve the actions which flow from this *love* in ourselves or others. This *disinterested affection* may appear strange to men impressed with notions of *self-love* as the sole motive of action—notions from the pulpit, the schools, the systems, and conversations regulated by them—but let us consider it in its strongest and simplest kinds; and when we see the possibility of it in these instances, we may easily discover its *universal extent*.

This is indeed a good metaphor; and wherever we find a determination among several rational agents toward mutual love, let each *individual* be looked upon as a *part* of a great *whole*, or *system*, and concern himself with the *public good* of it.

Now this proves *benevolence* to be in some degree extended to all mankind, where there is *no interfering interest*, which from *self-love* may obstruct it. And had we any notions of *rational agents*, capable of *moral affections*, on the *most distant* planets, our good *wishes* would still attend them, and we should delight in their happiness.

Section 2.
National Love.

XI. HERE we may briefly remark upon the foundation of what we call *national love*, or LOVE of one’s native *country*. In whatever place we have lived for any considerable time, we have most clearly observed various aspects of *human nature*; we have known many *lovely characters*; we remember the *associations*, *friendships*, *families*, *natural affections*, and other *human sentiments*. Our *moral sense* leads us to approve of these *lovely dispositions* where we have most clearly observed them, and our *benevolence* makes us concerned for the *interests* of the *persons* who possess them. When we come to observe the same things as clearly in another *country*, we begin to acquire a *national love* toward it as well; nor does our *own* country have any other preference in our *minds*, unless it is by an *association* of the pleasant *ideas* of our *youth* with the *buildings*, *fields*, and *woods* where we experienced them. This allows us to see how *tyranny*, *faction*, a *neglect* of *justice*, a *corruption* of *morals*, and anything that causes the *misery* of the *subjects* destroys this *national love* and the dear *idea* of a COUNTRY.

### SECTION III.

#### *The Sense of Virtue, and the various Opinions about it, reducible to one general Foundation. The Manner of computing the Morality of Actions.*

<margin>
All Virtue is Benevolent.
</margin>

I. IF we examine all the actions which are counted *amiable* anywhere, and inquire into the grounds upon which they are *approved*, we shall find that, in the opinion of the person who approves them, they always appear as BENEVOLENT, or flowing from *love of others* and a study of their happiness, whether the approver is one of the persons loved or benefited, or not; so that all those *kind affections* which incline us to make others happy, and all actions supposed to flow from *such affections*, appear *morally good*, if, while they are benevolent toward some persons, they are not harmful to others. Nor shall we find anything amiable in any action whatsoever where there is no benevolence imagined; nor in any disposition or capacity which is not supposed applicable to and designed for benevolent purposes. Nay, as was before observed*,

<margin>Religion.</margin>
II. HENCE *those* affections which would lead us to do good to our benefactor shall appear amiable, and the *contrary affections* odious, even when our actions cannot possibly be of any advantage or hurt to him. Thus a *sincere love* and *gratitude* toward our benefactor, a *cheerful readiness* to do whatever he shall require, however burdensome, a *hearty inclination* to comply with his intentions, and *contentment* with the *state* he has placed us in, are the strongest evidences of *benevolence* we can show to such a person; and therefore they must appear exceedingly amiable. And under these is included all the *rational devotion*, or *religion* toward a DEITY apprehended as *good*, which we can possibly perform.

WE may here briefly observe one circumstance in the *frame* of our *nature* which is wonderfully adapted to promote *benevolence*, namely, that as a benefit conferred necessarily raises *gratitude* in the

And let it be here observed, that we are so far from imagining all men to act only from *self-love*, that we universally expect in others a regard for the *public*; and do not look upon the want of this, as barely the absence of *moral good*, or *virtue*, but even as *positively evil and hateful*.

<margin>
The nature of a tyrant.
</margin>
The temper of a *tyrant* seems probably to be a continued state of *anger*, *hatred*, and *fear*. To form our judgment, then, of his motives of *action*, and those of men of similar tempers in lower positions, let us reflect upon the perceptions we form of mankind when we are under any of those passions which, to the *tyrant*, are *habitual*. When we are under the fresh impressions of an injury, we plainly find that our minds are wholly filled with perceptions of the person who injured us, as if he were *absolutely evil* and *delighted* in doing mischief. We overlook the virtues which, when calm, we could have observed in him; we forget that perhaps only *self-love*, and not *malice*, was his motive; or it may be some generous or kind intention toward others. These, probably, are the opinions which a *tyrant* constantly forms concerning *mankind*; and having very much weakened all kind *affections* in himself, however he may pretend to them, he judges the tempers of others by his own. <margin>*Ordinary Springs of Vice.*</margin>
The ordinary springs of vice then among men must be a *mistaken self-love*, made so violent as to overcome *benevolence*; or *affections* arising from false and rashly formed *opinions* of mankind, which we run into through the weakness of our *benevolence*. When men who had good opinions of each other happen to have contrary *interests*, they are apt to have their *good opinions* of each other diminished by imagining a *designed opposition* from *malice*; without this, they can scarcely hate one another. Thus two candidates for the same office wish each other dead, because that is an ordinary way by which men make room for each other; but if there remains any reflection on each other's *virtue*, as there sometimes may in benevolent tempers, then their *opposition* may be without *hatred*; and if another better *post*, where there is no *competition*, were

<margin>Self-love is indifferent.</margin>
V. The actions which flow solely from *self-love*, and yet show no lack of *benevolence*, having no hurtful effects upon others, seem *perfectly indifferent in a moral sense*, and neither raise the *love* nor *hatred* of the observer. Our reason can indeed discover certain bounds within which we may not only act from *self-love* consistently with the *good* of the *whole*, but every mortal's acting thus within these bounds for his own *good* is absolutely necessary for the *good* of the *whole*; and the lack of such *self-love* would be universally pernicious. Hence, he who pursues his own *private good* with an intention also to concur with that constitution which tends to the *good* of the *whole*; and much more he who promotes his *own good* with a direct view of making himself more capable of serving God or doing good to *mankind*, acts not only innocently, but also *honorably* and *virtuously*; for in both these cases, a motive of *benevolence* concurs with *self-love* to motivate him to the action. And thus a *neglect* of our *own good* may be *morally evil* and show a lack of *benevolence* toward the *whole*. But when *self-love* breaks over the bounds mentioned above and leads us into actions detrimen-

*Self-love* not excluded by *benevolence*.

<margin>*Benevolence, how affected by the qualities of its object.*</margin>
VII. THE moral *beauty* or *deformity* of *actions* is not altered by the *moral qualities* of the objects, any further than the qualities of the objects increase or diminish the benevolence of the *action*, or the *public good* intended by it. Thus *benevolence* toward the *worst characters*, or the study of their *good*, may be as amiable as any whatsoever; indeed, often more so than that toward the *good*, since it argues such a strong degree of benevolence as can surmount the greatest obstacle: the *moral evil* in the object. Hence the *love of unjust enemies* is counted among the highest *virtues*. <margin>Consequences, how they affect the morality of actions.</margin>
IX. AGAIN, when the *consequences* of *actions* are of a mixed nature, partly *advantageous* and partly *harmful*; that *action* is good, whose good effects outweigh the *evil* by being useful to many and harmful to few; and that *action* is *evil* which is otherwise. Here also the moral importance of *characters*, or the *dignity* of *persons*, may compensate for *numbers*; as may also the *degrees* of *happiness* or *misery*: for to procure an *insignificant good* for many, but an *immense evil* for a few, may be *evil*; and an *immense good* for a few may outweigh a *small evil* for many.

BUT the *consequences* which affect the *morality* of *actions* are not only the direct and natural *effects* of the *actions* themselves, but also all those *events* which otherwise would not have happened. For many *actions* which have no immediate or natural evil *effects*—nay, which actually produce good *effects*—may be *evil* if a man foresees that the evil *consequences* which will probably flow from the *folly* of others upon his performing such *actions* are so great as to outweigh all the *good* produced by those *actions*, or all the *evils* which would flow from the *omission* of them: And in such cases the

<page-num>179</page-num>

# MORAL GOOD and EVIL.

*Probability* is to be calculated on both sides. Sect. 3.
Thus, if an action of mine will probably, through the mistakes or corruption of others, be made a *precedent* in unlike cases for very evil *actions*; or when my *action*, though good in itself, will probably provoke men to very evil *actions* based on some *mistaken notion* of their right; any of these considerations, if foreseen by me, may make such an *action* of mine *evil* whenever the evils which will probably be caused by the *action* are greater than the evils caused by its *omission*.

AND this is the reason that many *laws* prohibit *actions* in general, even when some particular *instances* of those *actions* would be very useful; because an universal *allowance* of them, considering the mistakes men would probably fall into, would be more harmful than an universal *prohibition*; nor could there be any more *specific boundaries* fixed between the right and wrong cases. In such cases, it is the duty of persons to comply with the generally useful constitution; or if in some very important instances, the violation of the law would have less evil consequences than obedience to it, they must patiently resolve to undergo those penalties which the state has, for valuable ends to the whole, appointed: and this disobedience will have nothing criminal in it.

<sig>N 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: X. FROM</meta>
<vocab>Moral Good, Evil, Probability, Laws, State, Penalties, Disobedience, Constitution</vocab>

<summary>The author argues that an action is considered evil if it is likely to serve as a bad precedent or provoke others to do harm, even if the action itself seems good. He also justifies general laws as necessary safeguards, suggesting that while the law should generally be followed, civil disobedience is not criminal if it serves a greater good and the individual accepts the legal penalties.</summary>

Partial Benevolence, how virtuous.

X. FROM the last two *observations*, we may see what *actions* our *moral sense* would most recommend for our *choice* as the most *perfectly virtuous*: *namely*, those which appear to have the most universal, unlimited tendency toward the greatest and most *extensive happiness* of all the *rational agents* whom our influence can reach. All *benevolence*, even toward a *part*, is amiable when it is not inconsistent with the *good* of the *whole*: but this is a smaller degree of *virtue*, unless our beneficence is restrained by a lack of power, and not a lack of love for the *whole*. All strict attachments to *parties*, *sects*, or *factions* have but an imperfect *kind* of *beauty*, unless the *good* of the *whole* requires a stricter attachment to a *part*—as in *natural affection* or *virtuous friendships*—or when some *parts* are so eminently useful to the *whole* that even *universal benevolence* would lead us to study their *interests* with special care and affection. Thus, *universal benevolence* would incline us toward a stronger concern for the interests of great and generous *characters* in a high station, or make us more earnestly study the interests of any generous *society* whose entire constitution was designed to promote *universal good*. Likewise, a good taste in *architecture* would lead a man who was not able to afford the expense

<margin>
Moral dispositions and abilities.
</margin>
From this primary idea of moral good in actions, arises the idea of good in those dispositions, whether *natural* or *acquired*, which enable us to do good to others; or which are presumed to be designed, and acquired or cultivated for that purpose. And hence those abilities, while nothing appears contrary to our presumption, may increase our love to the possessor of them; but when they are imagined to be intended for *public mischief*, they make us hate him the more: Such are a *penetrating judgment, a tenacious memory, a quick invention; patience of labor, pain, hunger, watching; a contempt of wealth, rumor, death.* These may be rather called *natural abilities*, than *moral qualities*. Now, a veneration for these qualities, any further than they are employed for the *public good*, is foolish, and flows from our *moral sense*, grounded upon a false opinion; for if we plainly see them *maliciously employed*, they make the agent more detestable.

<meta>This page continues the introduction of a "universal canon" for computing the morality of actions, transitioning from the conceptual definition of virtue to a series of mathematical-style axioms used to calculate moral value based on benevolence and ability.</meta>

<page-num>183</page-num>
<header>MORAL GOOD and EVIL.</header>

we must observe the following *propositions* or *axioms* in Section 3.

1. THE *moral importance* of any *agent*, or the *quantity* of *public good* produced by him, is in a *compound ratio* of his *benevolence* and *abilities*: or (by substituting the initial letters for the words, as M = *moment* of *good*, and $\mu$ = *moment* of *evil*) M = B $\times$ A.

2. IN the same way, the *moment* of *private good*, or *interest* produced by any *person* for himself, is in a *compound ratio* of his *self-love* and *abilities*: or (substituting the initial letters) I = S $\times$ A.

3. WHEN, in comparing the *virtue* of two *actions*, the *abilities* of the *agents* are equal, the *moment* of *public good* produced by them in similar circumstances is proportional to the *benevolence*: or M = B $\times$ 1.

4. WHEN *benevolence* in two *agents* is equal, and other circumstances are the same, the *moment* of *public good* is proportional to the *abilities*: or M = A $\times$ 1.

5. THE *virtue* of *agents*, then, or their *benevolence*, is always *directly* proportional to the *moment* of *good* produced in similar circumstances, and *inversely* proportional to their *abilities*: or B = <gloss>M / A</gloss>

<sig>N 4</sig>
<meta>catchword: 6. But</meta>

<summary>This page presents five mathematical axioms for calculating morality, defining the quantity of public or private good as a product of an agent's benevolence (or self-love) and their abilities. It establishes that virtue is directly proportional to the good produced and inversely proportional to the agent's capacity.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the mathematical axioms for moral calculation, specifically introducing self-love and interest as variables that either assist or oppose benevolence in determining the value of an action.</meta>

<page-num>184</page-num>
<header>An INQUIRY concerning</header>

<margin>
Section 3.
</margin>

6. **B**ut as the natural consequences of our actions are various—some good to ourselves and evil to the public, and others evil to ourselves and good to the public; or even useful both to ourselves and others, or harmful to both—the entire motive for good actions is not always *benevolence alone*, nor is the motive for evil *malice alone* (indeed, this last is seldom a motive at all). Instead, in most actions, we must look upon *self-love* as another force, sometimes combining with *benevolence* and assisting it, when we are excited by views of *private interest* as well as public good; and sometimes opposing *benevolence*, when a good action is in any way difficult or painful to perform, or detrimental in its consequences to the agent. In the former case, M = (B + S) × A = BA + SA; and therefore BA = M - SA = M - I, and B = (M - I) / A. In the latter case, M = (B - S) × A = BA - SA; therefore BA = M + SA = M + I, and B = (M + I) / A.

**T**hese *selfish motives* shall be explained more fully hereafter; <note>See Section 5.</note> here we may generally denote them by the word *Interest*:

*interest*: which when it concurs with *benevolence*, in any *action* capable of *increase* or *diminution*, must produce a greater *quantity of good* than *benevolence* alone in the same *abilities*; and therefore when the *moment of good*, in an *action* partly intended for the *good of the agent*, is but equal to the *moment of good* in the *action* of another *agent*, influenced only by *benevolence*, the former is less *virtuous*; and in this *case* the *interest* must be deducted to find the true *effect* of the *benevolence*, or *virtue*. In the same manner, when *interest* is opposite to *benevolence*, and yet is overcome by it; this *interest* must be added to the *moment*, to increase the *virtue* of the *action*, or the *strength* of the *benevolence*: Or thus, in *advantageous* *virtue*, B = (M - I) / A. And in *laborious, painful, dangerous* or *expensive virtue*, B = (M + I) / A. By *interest*, in this last *case*, is understood all the *advantage* which the *agent* might have obtained by omitting the *action*, which is a *negative motive* to it; and this, when subtracted, becomes *positive*.

Indeed, *self-interest* then only diminishes the *benevolence* when, without this view of *interest*, the action would not have been undertaken, or so much *good* would not have been produced by the *agent*; and it extenuates the *vice* of an *evil action* only when, without this *interest*, the action would not have been pleasing to the *agent*, or so much *evil* would have been produced by him.

The sixth axiom only explains the external marks by which men must judge, who do not see into each other's hearts; for it may really happen in many cases that men may have *benevolence* sufficient to surmount any difficulty, and yet they may meet with none at all. And in that case, it is certain there is as much *virtue* in the *agent*, though he does not give such proof of it to his fellow creatures, as if he had surmounted difficulties in his kind actions. And this too must be the case with the DEITY, to whom nothing is difficult.

<margin>Perfect Virtue.</margin>
SINCE then *benevolence*, or *virtue* in any *agent*, is as M/A, or as (M+I)/A, and no *being* can act above his natural *ability*; that must be the perfection of *virtue* where M=A, or when the *being* acts to the utmost of his power for the public *good*; and hence the perfection of *virtue* in this case, or M/A, is as *unity*. <margin>Sect. 3.</margin>
It is true indeed, that *that public evil* which I neither certainly foresee, nor have *actual presumptions* of, as the consequence of my *action*, does not make my present *action criminal* or *odious*; even although I might have foreseen this *evil* by a serious *examination* of my own *actions*; because such *actions* do not, at present, evidence either *malice* or a *lack of benevolence*. But then it is also certain that my prior *negligence* in not examining the *tendency* of my *actions* is plain *evidence* of the lack of *that degree* of good *affections* which is necessary to a *virtuous character*; and consequently, the *guilt* properly lies in this *neglect* rather than in an *action* which really flows from a good *intention*. *Human laws*, however, which cannot examine the *intentions* or secret *knowledge* of the *agent*, must judge the *action* itself in general, presupposing all that *knowledge* as actually attained which we are obliged to attain.

In like manner, no good *effect* which I did not actually foresee and intend makes my *action* morally *good*, however *human laws* or *governors*, who cannot search into *men's intentions* or know their

If it be said, “That actions from *instinct* are not the effect of *prudence* and *choice*,” this objection holds just as strongly against the actions which flow from *self-love*; since the use of our reason is as necessary to find the proper means of promoting *public good* as *private good*. And as it must be an *instinct*, or a determination prior to *reason*, which makes us pursue *private good* as well as *public good* as our end, there is the same occasion for *prudence* and *choice* in the election of proper means for promoting either. I see no harm in supposing “that men are *naturally disposed* to *virtue*, and not left *merely indifferent*, to be engaged in actions only as they appear to tend to their own *private good*.” Surely, the supposition of a benevolent *universal instinct* would recommend *human nature* and its AUTHOR more to the *love* of a *good* man, and leave room enough for the exercise of our *reason* in contriving and settling *rights*, *laws*, and *constitutions*; in inventing arts and practicing them so as to gratify that generous inclination in the most effective manner. And if we must bring in *self-love* to make *virtue rational*, a

<meta>catchword: little</meta>

<summary>The author argues that virtue based on instinct is no less rational than virtue based on self-love, as both require reason to determine the best means to achieve their ends. He posits that a natural, universal benevolent instinct reflects more favorably on human nature and its Creator than a system where humans are only motivated by private interest.</summary>

Little reflection will discover, as shall appear hereafter, that this *benevolence* is our greatest *happiness*; and from this we may resolve to cultivate this sweet *disposition* as much as possible, and to despise every opposite *interest*. This does not mean we can be truly *virtuous* if we intend only to obtain the pleasure that accompanies *beneficence* without the *love of others*: In fact, this very pleasure is founded on our being conscious of *disinterested love to others* as the *spring* of our actions. But *self-interest* may be our motive in choosing to continue in this agreeable state, though it cannot be the sole or principal motive of any action which appears *virtuous* to our *moral sense*.

At present, we shall only draw this one conclusion, which seems the most joyful imaginable even to the lowest rank of mankind, namely: "That no external circumstances of fortune, no involuntary disadvantages, can exclude any mortal from the *most heroic virtue*." ### SECT. IV.

### *All Mankind agree in this general Foundation of their Approbation of moral Actions. The Grounds of the different Opinions about Morals.*

---

<margin>*This moral sense is universal.*</margin>
I. To show how far mankind agrees in what we have established as the *universal foundation* of this *moral sense*, namely, BENEVOLENCE, we have observed already <note>See above, Section 3, Article 3, Paragraph 3.</note>, that when we are asked the reason for our approval of any action, we perpetually cite its *usefulness* to the *public*, and not to the *actor* himself. If we are vindicating a censured action and maintaining it as lawful, we always make this one point of our defense,
> “ That
> “ it injured nobody, or did more *good*
> “ than *harm*.”

On the other hand, when we blame any piece of conduct, we show it to be prejudicial to others besides the *actor*; or to evidence at least a *neglect* of their interest when it was in our power to serve them; or when *gratitude*, *natural affection*, or some other *disinterested bond*

should have raised in us a concern for their interest. If we sometimes blame foolish conduct in others, without any reflection upon its tendency to *public evil*, it is still occasioned by our *benevolence*, which makes us concerned for the evils befalling the *agent*, whom we must always look upon as a part of the *system*. We all know how great an extenuation of crimes it is to allege, "That the poor man does harm to nobody but himself;" and how often this turns hatred into *pity*. And yet if we examine the matter well, we shall find that the greatest part of the actions which are *immediately* prejudicial to ourselves, and are often looked upon as *innocent* toward others, do really tend to the *public detriment*, by making us incapable of performing the good deeds we could otherwise have done, and perhaps would have been inclined to do. This is the case of *intemperance* and *extravagant luxury*.

<margin>Benevolence the sole ground of Approbation.</margin>
II. AND further, we may observe that no action of any other person was ever approved by us, but upon some perception, well or ill grounded, of some really good moral *quality*. If we observe the sentiments of men concerning actions, we shall find that it is always some *really amiable and benevolent appearance* which engages their approbation. And this *sense*, like our other *senses*, though counteracted by motives of external *advantage* which are stronger than it, does not cease to operate, but has strength enough to make us *uneasy* and *dissatisfied* with ourselves; just as the *sense of taste* makes us loathe and dislike the nauseous potion which we may force ourselves, out of *interest*, to swallow.

<margin>Sect. 4.</margin>
The matter is plainly this. Men are often mistaken in the tendency of actions toward either *public* or *private good*: indeed, sometimes violent *passions*, while they last, will make them approve of very bad actions in a *moral sense*, or very pernicious ones to the *agent* as being *advantageous*. But this proves only, "that sometimes there may be some more *violent motive* to *action* than a *sense of moral good*; or that men, through *passion*, may become blind even to their own *interest*."

But to prove that men are void of a *moral sense*, we should find some *instances* of cruel, malicious *actions* performed and approved in others when there is no *motive* of *interest*, real or apparent, except gratifying that very *desire for mischief* toward others. We must find a country where *murder in cold blood*, *tortures*, and everything malicious—without any *advantage*—is, if not approved, at least looked upon with indifference and raises no *aversion* toward the *actors* in unconcerned *spectators*. We must find men by whom the *treacherous*, *ungrateful*, and *cruel* are held in the same regard as the *generous*, *friendly*, *faithful*, and *humane*; and who approve the latter no more than the *former* in all cases where they are not affected by the influence of these *dispositions*, or when the *natural good* or *evil* befalls other

### Section 4. Practices which prevail in the world are much better arguments that men lack *reason* than that they lack a *moral sense of beauty in actions*.

---

<margin>
Narrow systems pervert the moral sense.
</margin>

IV. THE next basis for the *diversity* of *sentiments* is the *diversity of systems* to which men, due to foolish *opinions*, restrict their *benevolence*. We suggested earlier\*, that it is natural and beautiful to feel a stronger *benevolence* toward the *morally good* parts of mankind—those who are *useful to the whole*—than toward those who are *useless or harmful*. Now, if men adopt a low or base *opinion* of any *group* or *sect* of people; if they imagine them intent on the destruction of the more valuable parts of society, or regard them as merely *useless burdens* on the earth; *benevolence* itself will lead them to neglect the *interests* of such people and to suppress them. This is the *reason* why, among nations that hold high notions of *virtue*, every *action* toward an enemy may be seen as *just*; and why the ROMANS and GREEKS could approve of making those they called *barbarians* into *slaves*.

<margin>
Sects harmful to virtue.
</margin>
