Men begin to hate each other for what, in itself, has no evil in it; and to love the *zealots* of their own *sect* for what is in no way valuable; indeed, even for their *fury*, *rage*, and *malice* against opposite *sects* (which is what all *parties* commonly call *zeal*). It is then no wonder if our *moral sense* is much impaired, and our *natural notions* of *good* and *evil* almost lost, when our *admiration* and *love*, or *contempt* and *hatred*, are thus perverted from their natural objects.

If any mortals are so happy as never to have heard of the *party-tenets* of most of our *sects*; or if they have heard of them, have either never espoused any *sect*, or all equally; they stand the best chance for a truly *natural* and *good disposition*, because their *tempers* have never been soured about vain *trifles*; nor have they contracted any *sullenness* or *rancor* against any part of their own kind. “*selfish Views.*” This wise *philosophy* of some *moderns*, following EPICURUS, must be productive of nothing but *discontent*, *suspicion*, and *jealousy*; a state infinitely worse than any small, transitory *injuries* to which we might be exposed by a good-natured *credulity*. Were we freely to converse with *robbers*, who show a *moral sense* in the equal or *proportional division* of their *prey*, and in *faith* to each other, we would find they have their own sublime *moral ideas* of their party as *generous*, *courageous*, *trusty*, and even *honest* too; and that those we call *honest* and *industrious* are imagined by them to be *mean-spirited*, *selfish*, *churlish*, or *luxurious*. Perhaps no men ever pursued vice for long with peace of mind without some such deluding imagination of *moral good* <note>See below, Section 6, Article 2, Paragraph 1.</note>, while they may still be unaware of the barbarous and inhuman consequences of their actions. The idea of an *ill-natured villain* is too frightful to ever become familiar to any mortal. Hence we shall find that the *basest actions* are dressed in some tolerable mask. What others call *avarice* appears to the *agent* as a *prudent care of a family or friends*; *fraud* as *artful conduct*; *malice and revenge* as a *just sense of honor* and a *vindication of our right to possessions or fame*; *fire and sword* and *desolation* among *enemies* as a *just, thorough defense of our country*; *persecution* as a *zeal for the truth* and for the eternal happiness of men, which *heretics* oppose. <margin>False opinions of the divine laws.</margin>
V. THE last ground of *diversity* which occurs is the *false opinions* of the *will* or *laws* of the DEITY. To obey these, we are determined by *gratitude* and a *sense of right* imagined in the DEITY to dispose of the fortunes of his creatures at his pleasure. This is so widely known to have produced *follies, superstitions, murders, and the devastation of kingdoms* out of a *sense of virtue and duty*, that it is needless to mention particular instances. We may only observe, “that all those *follies* or *barbarities* rather confirm than destroy the opinion of a *moral sense*”; since the DEITY is believed to have a *right* to dispose of his creatures; and *gratitude* to him, if he is conceived as *good*, must move us to obedience to his *will*: if he is not conceived as *good*, *self-love* may overcome our *moral sense* of the action we undertake to avoid his fury.

### Sect. 4.

As for the vices which commonly proceed from *love of pleasure*, or any violent *passion*, since generally the *agent* is soon aware of their *evil*—and sometimes even in the heat of the action—they only prove, “That this moral *sense* and *benevolence* may be overcome by the more urgent demands of other *desires*.”

<margin>Objection from *incest*.</margin>
VI. THE answer to this may be easily found from what has already been said. Had we no *moral sense* natural to us, we would only look upon *incest* as hurtful to ourselves and avoid it; we would never hate other *incestuous persons* any more than we do a *bankrupt merchant*. And further, it is true that many who abhor *incest* do not know or reflect upon the natural tendency of some types of *incest* toward the *public detriment*; but wherever it is hated, it is perceived as offensive to the DEITY, and it is believed that it exposes the person concerned to His just vengeance. Now, it is universally acknowledged to be the grossest ingratitude and baseness for any creature to counteract the will of the DEITY, to whom it is under such obligations. This, then, is plainly a *moral evil quality* perceived in *incest*, and can be reduced to the general foundation of *malice*, or rather, a *lack of benevolence*. But without a *moral sense*, we could receive no prejudice against actions under any other view than as *naturally disadvantageous* to ourselves.

### Section 5. An increase of *Love*, according as the Object is more nearly attached to us, or our *Friends*, by good deeds which affect *ourselves*, or *them*.

<image-desc>A decorative horizontal flourish composed of three wavy, calligraphic lines.</image-desc>

**This** *universal benevolence* toward all men, we may compare to that principle of *gravitation*, which perhaps extends to all bodies in the *universe*; but, like the *love* of *benevolence*, it increases as the distance is diminished, and is *strongest* when bodies come to touch each other. Now, this *increase* of *attraction* upon a nearer approach is as necessary to the *structure* of the *universe* as the existence of any *attraction* at all. For a general *attraction*, equal at all distances, would—by the opposition of such multitudes of equal forces—put an end to all regularity of motion, and perhaps stop it altogether.

**This** *increase* of *love* toward the *benevolent*, according to their nearer *approaches* to *ourselves* by their benefits, is observable in the high degree of *love* which *heroes* and *lawgivers* universally obtain in their own countries, above what they find abroad, even among those who are not insensitive to their virtues; and in all the strong ties of *friendship*, *acquaintance*, *neighborhood*, and *partnership*, which are exceedingly necessary to the order and happiness of human society.

<meta>catchword: III. FROM</meta>

<summary>The author compares universal benevolence to gravitation, noting that both must increase in strength as the distance between objects or people decreases to maintain order. This natural tendency to favor those closer to us—such as friends, neighbors, or national heroes—is presented as essential for a functioning and happy society.</summary>

<margin>Love of Honor.</margin>
III. FROM considering that strong *determination* in our *nature* toward *gratitude* and *love* for our *benefactors*, which was already shown to be *disinterested*\* ; we are easily led to consider *another determination* of our *minds*, equally natural with the former, which is to delight in the good *opinion* and *love of others*, even when we expect no other *advantage* from them, except what flows from *this constitution*, by which HONOUR is made an immediate *good*. I would call this desire for honor AMBITION, had not *custom* joined some evil ideas to that word, making it signify such a violent desire for honor and power that it would make us stop at no base means to obtain them. On the other hand, we are by NATURE subjected to a painful *sensation* of *misery* from the unfavorable opinions others have of us, even when we dread no other *evil* from them. This we call SHAME, which is constituted as an immediate *evil* in the same way we said *honor* was an immediate *good*.

NOW, if there were no *moral sense*, or if we had no other idea of *actions* except as *advantageous* or *hurtful*, I see no reason why we should be delighted by *honor*, or sub-

--- Page 256 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of shame, specifically arguing that the pain of shame is distinct from the fear of material loss or damaged reputation regarding one's business capacity.</meta>

<page-num>222</page-num>
<header>An INQUIRY concerning</header>

<margin>Sect. 5.</margin>
jected to the *uneasiness* of *shame*; or how it could ever happen that a man who is secure from punishment for any action should ever be *uneasy* at its being known to *all the world*. The *world* may have a worse opinion of him for it; but what subjects my ease to the opinion of the *world*? Why, perhaps we shall not be so much trusted in business from now on, and so suffer loss. If this is the only reason for *shame*, and it has no immediate *evil* or *pain* in it distinct from the fear of loss, then whenever we expose ourselves to loss we should be *ashamed* and endeavor to conceal the action; and yet it is quite otherwise.

A MERCHANT, for instance, lest it should impair his credit, conceals a *shipwreck* or a very bad *market* to which he has sent his goods. But is this the same as the passion of SHAME? Does he have that *anguish*, that *dejection of mind*, and *self-condemnation* which one has whose treachery is detected? Nay, how men will sometimes glory in their losses when in a cause imagined to be morally good, though they really weaken their credit in the merchant's sense—that is, the opinion of their *wealth* or *fitness* for business? Was any man ever ashamed of impoverishing himself to serve his *country* or his *friend*?

<meta>catchword: IV. THE</meta>

<summary>The author distinguishes the psychological pain of shame from the practical fear of financial loss, noting that moral treachery produces a unique self-condemnation not felt in business failures. He highlights that people may even take pride in financial ruin if it results from a morally virtuous act, such as serving a friend or country.</summary>

<meta>This page transitions from a discussion on the distinct nature of shame to an exploration of whether the opinions of one's country or the desire for honor serve as the foundational standard for moral virtue.</meta>

<page-num>223</page-num>
<header>MORAL GOOD and EVIL. Sect. 5.</header>

<margin>The foundation of morals is not the opinions of our country.</margin>

IV. THE opinions of our country are made by some the first standard of *virtue*. They allege, “That by comparing actions to them, we first distinguish between *moral good and evil*: And then,” they say, “AMBITION, or the *love of* HONOR, is our chief *motive*.” But what is *honor*? It is not just being universally known, no matter how. A *covetous man* is not *honored* by being universally known as *covetous*; nor a *weak, selfish*, or *luxurious man*, when he is known to be so: much less can a *treacherous, cruel*, or *ungrateful man* be said to be *honored* for being known as such. A *posture-master* <note>a contortionist or acrobat</note>, a *fire-eater*, or practitioner of *legerdemain* <note>sleight-of-hand</note>, is not *honored* for these public shows, unless we consider him as a person capable of giving the pleasures of *admiration* and *surprise* to multitudes. *Honor*, then, is the *opinion of others concerning our morally good actions, or abilities presumed to be applied that way*; for *abilities* constantly applied to other purposes procure the greatest infamy. Now, it is certain that *ambition*, or the *love of honor*, is really *selfish*; but then this determination to love *honor* presupposes a *sense of moral virtue*, both in the persons who confer the honor and in him who pursues it.

->AND<-

<summary>The author argues that honor is not merely being well-known, but is specifically the positive opinion others hold regarding one's moral actions or beneficial abilities. He concludes that the pursuit of honor (ambition) actually depends upon a pre-existing moral sense in both the individual and society.</summary>
<keywords>virtue, moral good, evil, ambition, honor, motive, reputation, legerdemain, infamy, moral sense</keywords>

## Sect. 5.

AND let it be observed that if we knew an *agent* had no other motive for action than *ambition*, we would perceive no virtue even in his most useful actions, since they did not flow from any *love* for others or *desire* for their *happiness*. When *honor* is thus constituted by NATURE as *pleasant* to us, it may be an additional *motive* for *virtue*, just as we said above <note>See Section 3, Article 15, Paragraph 2.</note> that the *pleasure* arising from *reflection* on our *benevolence* was. However, the *person* whom we imagine as *perfectly virtuous* acts immediately from the *love* of others, even though these refined *interests* may be *joint motives* for him to begin such a *course* of actions, or to cultivate every kind of *inclination* and to despise every contrary *interest* as providing less happiness than *reflection* on his own virtue and *consciousness* of the *esteem* of others.

SHAME is in the same manner constituted as an immediate *evil*, and it influences us in the same way to abstain from *moral evil*; not that any action or omission would appear virtuous where the sole motive was fear of shame.

<margin>
Opinions flow from the moral sense.

If an *action* is believed to be *advantageous* to the *agent*, we may be led to believe so too, and then *self-love* may make us undertake it; or it may, in the same way, make us shun an *action* reputed to be *harmful* to the *agent*. If an *action* is considered *advantageous* to the *public*, we may believe so too; and what next? If we have no *disinterested benevolence*, what shall move us to undertake it?

"Why," one might say, "we love *honor*; and to obtain this pleasure, we will undertake the *action* out of *self-interest*." Now, is *honor* only the opinion of our country that an *action* is *advantageous* to the *public*? No: we see no *honor* paid to the *useful treachery* of an *enemy* whom we have bribed to our side, to *accidental unintended services*, or to the most *useful effects* of *compelling* *cowards* to act; and yet we see *honor* paid to *unsuccessful attempts* to serve the *public* out of sincere love for it. *Honor* then presupposes a *sense* of something *lovable* besides *advantage*, namely, a *sense* of *excellence* in a *public spirit*; and therefore the *initial sense* of *moral good* must exist prior to *honor*, for *honor* is founded upon it. <margin>Moral sense, not from love of honor.</margin>
VI. To explain what has been said of the power of *honor*. Suppose a STATE or PRINCE, observing the money which is drawn out of *England* by *Italian musicians*, should decree *honors, statues, titles*, for great *musicians*: this would certainly excite all who had hopes of success to the study of *music*; and men of a good ear would approve of the good performers as useful subjects, as well as very entertaining. But would this give all men a *good ear*, or make them delight in *harmony*? Or could it ever make us really love a *musician* who studied nothing but his own gain, in the same manner we do a *patriot* or a *generous friend*? I think not. And yet *friendship*, without the assistance of statues or honors, can make persons appear exceedingly amiable.

LET us take another instance. Suppose statues and triumphal arches were decreed, as well as a *large sum of money*, to the discoverer of the *longitude*, or any other useful invention in *mathematics*: this would raise a universal desire for such knowledge from *self-love*; but would men therefore love a *mathematician* as they do a *virtuous man*? THIS will show us with what judgment a recent * author compares the origin of our ideas of *Virtue*, and our approval of it, to *the manner of regulating the behavior of awkward children by commendation.* It shall appear later†, that our *approval* of certain *gestures*, and what we call *decency* in movement, depends upon some *moral ideas* in people of advanced years. But before children come to observe this relationship, it is only *good nature*, an *inclination* to please, and *love* of *praise*, which makes them endeavor to behave as they are desired; and not any perception of *excellence* in this behavior. Hence they are not solicitous about gestures when alone, unless with a view to please when they return to company; nor do they ever love or approve of others for any perfection of this kind, but rather envy or hate them; until they either discern the connection between *gestures* and *moral qualities*, or reflect on the *good nature*, which is evidenced by such a compliance with the desire of the company.

<header>MORAL GOOD and EVIL. Sect. 5.</header>

<margin>*False Honor.*</margin>
VII. THE considering of *Honor* in the manner explained above may show us the reason why men are often *ashamed* of things which are not *vicious*, and *honored* for what is not *virtuous*. For, if any action only appears *vicious* to any persons or company, although it is not so, they will have a bad idea of the *agent*; and then he may be ashamed, or suffer uneasiness in being thought *morally evil*. In the same way, those who look upon an action as *morally good* will honor the *agent*, and he may be pleased with the *honor*, although he does not himself perceive any *moral good* in what has procured it.

<margin>*Moral incapacity, a matter of shame.*</margin>
AGAIN, we shall be *ashamed* of every evidence of *moral incapacity* or lack of *ability*; and with good reason, when this lack is caused by our own negligence. Nay further, if any circumstance is looked upon as *indecent* in any country, *offensive* to others, or *deformed*, we shall, out of our love for the good opinions of others, be *ashamed* to be found in such circumstances, even when we are aware that this indecency or offense is not founded in *nature* but is merely the effect of *custom*. Thus, being observed in those functions of nature which are counted *indecent* and *offensive* will make us uneasy, although we are aware that they really do

This is the reason that *wealth* and *power*, the great *engines* of *virtue*, when presumed to be intended for benevolent purposes, either toward our friends or our country, procure *honor* from others, and are apt to beget *pride* in the *possessor*; which, as it is a general passion which may be either good or evil, according to how it is grounded, we may describe as the *joy* which arises from the real or imagined *possession* of *honor*, or *claim* to it. The same are the effects of *knowledge*, *sagacity*, and *strength*; and hence it is that *men* are apt to boast of them.

BUT whenever it appears that *men* have only their *private advantage* in view in the application of these *abilities*, or *natural advantages*, the *honor* ceases, and we study to conceal them, or at least are not fond of displaying them; and much more so when there is any suspicion of an *ill-natured* application. Selfishness shameful.

In short, we always see *actions* which flow from *public love* accompanied with generous boldness and openness; and not only malicious, but even *selfish ones*, are the matter of shame and confusion, and men study to conceal them. The love of *private pleasure* is the ordinary occasion of *vice*; and when men have acquired any lively notions of *virtue*, they generally begin to be ashamed of everything which betrays *selfishness*, even in instances where it is innocent. We are apt to imagine that others observing us in such pursuits form mean opinions of us, as being too much set on private pleasure; and hence we shall find such enjoyments, in most polite nations, concealed from those who do not partake with us. Such are venereal *pleasures* between *persons* married, and even eating and drinking alone any nicer sorts of meats or drinks; whereas a hospitable table is rather a matter of boasting; and so are all other kind, generous offices between married *persons*, where there is no suspicion of *self-love* in the *agent*, but he is imagined as acting from love to his *associate*. <margin>Honor and shame often arise from some associations of ideas.</margin>
HERE too we may see the reason why we are not ashamed of any of the methods of *grandeur* or *high-living*. There is such a mixture of *moral ideas*, of *benevolence*, of *abilities* kindly employed; so many *dependents supported*, so many *friends entertained*, *assisted*, *protected*; such an *imagined capacity* for great and amiable *actions*, that we are never ashamed, but rather boast of such things: We never seek obscurity or concealment, but rather desire that our state and magnificence should be known. Were it not for this conjunction of *moral ideas*, no mortal could bear the drudgery of state, or abstain from laughing at those who did. Could any man be pleased with a company of *statues* surrounding his table, so artfully contrived as to consume his various courses, and inspired by some servant, like so many puppets, to give the usual trifling returns in praise of their fare? Or with so many *machines* to perform the bows and whispers of a levee?

THE shame we suffer from the meanness of *dress*, *table*, *equipage*, is entirely owing to the same reason. This meanness is often imagined to argue *avarice*, *meanness*

To confirm this, let us observe that men will glory in the meanness of their food when it was occasioned by a *good action*. How many would be ashamed to be surprised at a dinner of *cold meat*, who will boast of having fed upon *dogs* and *horses* at the *Siege* of *Derry*? And they will all tell you that they were not, nor are they, ashamed of it.

**This** ordinary connection in our imagination between external *grandeur*, *regularity* in *dress*, *equipage*, *retinue*, *badges of honor*, and some *moral abilities* greater than ordinary, is perhaps of more consequence in the world than some recluse *philosophers* realize, who pride themselves on despising these external shows. This may possibly be a great, if not the only, cause of what some consider *miraculous*: namely, that *civil governors* of no greater capacity than their neighbors, through some inexpressible *awe* and *authority*, quell the spirits of the *common people* and keep them in subjection with such small guards as might easily be conquered by those *associations* which might be raised among the *disaffected* or *factious* members of any *state*; who are daring enough among their *equals* and show a sufficient contempt for *death* to undertake such an enterprise.

<meta>This page continues the argument regarding the social and moral perceptions of human behavior, shifting from the connection between wealth and character to the public nature of intellectual and aesthetic pleasures.</meta>

<page-num>236</page-num>
<header>*An* INQUIRY *concerning*</header>

Section 5.
<image-desc>A horizontal decorative flourish/squiggle.</image-desc>
</margin>

FROM THIS also we may discover the reason why gratifying our *superior senses* of *beauty* and *harmony*, or enjoying the pleasures of *knowledge*, never causes any shame or confusion, even if our enjoyment were known to the whole *world*. The objects which provide this pleasure are of such a nature as to afford the same delights to many people; nor is there anything in one person's enjoyment that excludes any other mortal from a similar enjoyment. So that although we pursue these enjoyments from *self-love*, yet, since our enjoyment cannot be harmful to others, no man is thought to be in any way *inhumanly selfish* from the fullest possible enjoyment of them. The same *regularity* or *harmony* which delights me may at the same time delight many others; the same *theorem* shall be equally productive of pleasure once it has entertained thousands. Men, therefore, are not ashamed of such pursuits, since they never, by themselves, seduce us into anything *malicious*, *envious*, or *mean-spirited*; nor does anyone perceive another as too *selfish* for pursuing objects of inexhaustible universal pleasure.

THIS view of *honor* and *shame* may also let us see the reason why most men are uneasy at being praised when they themselves are present. Everyone is delighted

Compassion a motive to virtue.
</margin>
VIII. Let us next consider another determination of our *mind*, which strongly proves *benevolence* to be natural to us, and that is **COMPASSION**; by which we are disposed to study the *interest* of others, without any views of *private advantage*. This needs little illustration. Every mortal is made uneasy by any grievous misery he sees another involved in, unless the person is imagined to be *evil*, in a *moral sense*: Indeed, it is almost impossible for us to be unmoved, even in that case. *Advantage* may make us perform a cruel action, or may overcome *pity*; but it scarcely ever extinguishes it. A sudden passion of *hatred* or *anger* may represent a person as *absolutely evil*, and so extinguish *pity*; but when the passion is over, it often returns. Another *disinterested view* may even in cold blood overcome *pity*; such as *love for our country*, or *zeal for religion*. *Persecution* is generally occasioned by the *love of virtue*, and

And if we see that this is impossible, we may by *reflection* discern it to be useless for us to indulge our *compassion* any further; and then *self-love* prompts us to withdraw from the object which causes our pain and to endeavor to divert our thoughts. But where there is no such *reflection*, people are hurried by a natural, kind *instinct* to see objects of *compassion* and expose themselves to this pain when they can give no reason for it, as in the instance of public *executions*.

**This** same principle leads men to *tragedies*; only we are to observe that another strong reason for this is the *moral beauty* of the *characters* and *actions* which we love to behold. For I doubt whether any audience would be pleased to see fictitious scenes of misery if they were kept strangers to the *moral qualities* of the sufferers, or their *characters* and *actions*. Since in such a case there would be no beauty to raise a desire to see such representations, I imagine we would not expose ourselves to pain alone from misery which we knew to be fictitious.

**It** was the same cause which crowded the *Roman theaters* to see *gladiators*. There

Meanwhile, they were oblivious to the fact that their crowding to see such sights—and their favoring of the people who presented them with these spectacles of courage and opportunities to follow their *natural instinct toward compassion*—was the true cause of all the real distress or assaults that they regretted.

**What** sentiments can we imagine a candidate would have raised about himself if he had presented his countrymen only with scenes of *misery*; if he had drained *hospitals* and *infirmaries* of all their pitiable inhabitants, or if he had bound many *slaves* and, without any resistance, butchered them with his own hands? <margin>*Compassion is natural.*</margin>
How independent this disposition toward *compassion* is of *custom*, *education*, or *instruction* will appear from its prevalence in *women* and *children*, who are less influenced by these factors. That *children* delight in some actions that are cruel and tormenting to *animals* in their power does not flow from *malice* or a lack of *compassion*, but from their *ignorance* of the signs of pain that many creatures make, together with a *curiosity* to see the various contortions of their bodies. For when they become more acquainted with these creatures, or come by any means to know their sufferings, their *compassion* often becomes too strong for their *reason*; as it generally does when they behold *executions*, where as soon as they observe the evidence of distress or pain in the *malefactor*, they are apt to condemn this necessary method of self-defense for the state.

# S E C T. VI.

## Concerning the Importance of this moral Sense to the present Happiness of Mankind, and its Influence on human Affairs.

<margin>Importance of the Moral Sense. I.</margin>
It may now probably appear that, notwithstanding the corruption of manners so justly complained of everywhere, this *moral sense* has a greater influence on mankind than is generally imagined, although it is often directed by very partial, imperfect views of *public good*, and often overcome by *self-love*. But we shall offer some further considerations to prove "that it gives us more *pleasure* and *pain* than all our other *faculties*." And to prevent repetitions, let us observe "that wherever any *morally good quality* gives pleasure from *reflection* or from *honor*, the contrary *evil one* will give proportionate *pain* from *remorse* and *shame*." Now we shall consider the *moral pleasures* not only separately but as they are the *most delightful ingredient* in the ordinary *pleasures* of *life*.

<summary>The author introduces an argument that the moral sense exerts a more profound influence on human happiness than is typically recognized, asserting that moral pleasures and pains outweigh all other faculties. He proposes to examine these moral feelings both on their own and as essential components of the common pleasures of life.</summary>
<keywords>moral sense, happiness, public good, self-love, pleasure and pain, reflection, honor, remorse, shame</keywords>

--- Page 277 ---
<meta>This page continues the investigation into the importance of the moral sense, asserting that humans naturally value moral excellence over all other forms of enjoyment and view moral evil as the greatest misery.</meta>

<header>MORAL GOOD and EVIL. Sect. 6.</header>
<page-num>243</page-num>

ALL MEN seem persuaded of some excellence in the possession of *good moral qualities*, which is superior to all other enjoyments; and on the contrary, they look upon a state of *moral evil* as worse and more wretched than any other whatsoever. We must not form our judgment in this matter from the actions of men; for however they may be influenced by *moral sentiments*, yet it is certain that *self-interested passions* frequently overcome them, and *partial views of the tendency of actions* make us do what is really morally evil, believing it to be *good*. But let us examine the sentiments which men universally form regarding the state of others, when they are in no way immediately concerned; for in these sentiments *human nature* is *calm* and *undisturbed*, and shows its true face.

Now should we imagine a *rational creature* in a sufficiently happy state, though his mind was, without *interruption*, wholly occupied with pleasant sensations of *smell, taste, touch, etc.* if at the same time all other ideas were excluded? Should we not think the state *low, mean,* and *sordid*, if there were no *society*, no *love* or *friendship*, no *kind acts*? What then must that state be wherein there are no pleasures but those of the *external senses*, with such long intervals as *human nature* at present

Castle-builders prove it.

What *castle-builder*, who forms for himself imaginary scenes of life in which he thinks he would be happy, ever made *acknowledged treachery*, *cruelty*, or *ingratitude* the steps by which he mounted to his wished-for elevation, or parts of his character when he had attained it? We always conduct ourselves in such reveries according to the dictates of *honor*, *faith*, *generosity*, and *courage*; and the lowest we can sink is hoping we may be enriched by some innocent accident.

II. Let us in the same manner examine our sentiments about the happiness of others in common life. **Wealth** and **external pleasures** take up no small space in our imaginations; but does there not always accompany this opinion of happiness in *wealth* some supposed kind intention of doing good deeds for persons dear to *us*, or at least to our *families* or *relatives*? III. There is a further consideration which must not be passed over, concerning the EXTERNAL BEAUTY of persons, which everyone agrees has a great power over human minds. Now it is some perceived *morality*, some natural or imagined indication of accompanying *virtue*, which gives it this powerful charm above all other kinds of *beauty*. Let us consider the characters of beauty which are commonly admired in countenances, and we shall find them to be *sweetness*, *mildness*, *majesty*, *dignity*, *vivacity*, *humility*, *tenderness*, and *good nature*; that is, that certain *airs*, *proportions*, and *je ne sais quoi* are natural indications of such virtues, or of abilities or dispositions toward them. As we observed above <note>See Section 5, Article 8, Paragraph 2.</note> regarding *misery* or *distress* appearing in countenances; so it is certain that almost all habitual *dispositions* of mind shape the countenance in such a manner as to give some indications of them to the spectator. Our violent passions are obvious at first sight in the countenance, so that sometimes no art can conceal them; and smaller degrees of them give less obvious turns to the face, which an accurate eye will observe. THIS consideration may show us one reason, among many others, for men's different *fancies* or *tastes* for beauty. The mind of man, however generally disposed to esteem *benevolence* and *virtue*, yet by more particular attention to some

Now, as the *various* tempers of men make *various* tempers of others agreeable to them, so they must differ in their *relishes* of *beauty*, according to how it denotes the several *qualities* most agreeable to themselves.

This may also show us how in *virtuous love* there may be the greatest beauty, without the least charm to engage a rival. *Love* itself gives a beauty to the lover, in the eyes of the person *beloved*, which no other mortal is much affected by. And this perhaps is the *strongest charm* possible, and that which will have the greatest power, where there is not some very great counter-balance from *wordly interest*, *vice*, or *gross deformity*.

### Sect. 6.

---

<margin>
Air, Motion, Gestures.
</margin>

IV. This same consideration may be extended to the whole *air* and *motion* of any person. Everything we count agreeable somehow denotes *cheerfulness, ease, a condescension,* and *readiness* to oblige, a *love of company,* along with a *freedom* and *boldness* which always accompanies an honest, undesigning heart. On the contrary, what is shocking in *air* or *motion* is *roughness, ill-nature,* a *disregard* for others, or a *foolish bashfulness,* which evidences a person to be inexperienced in society or the duties of humanity.

With relation to these *airs, motions, and gestures,* we may observe that, considering the different *ceremonies* and *modes* of showing respect which are practiced in different nations, we may indeed probably conclude that there is no *natural connection* between any of these *gestures* or *motions* and the *affections of the mind* which they are made to express by *custom*. But when *custom* has made any of them pass as *expressions* of such *affections* through a constant *association of ideas,* some shall become agreeable and lovely, and others extremely offensive, although they were both, in their own nature, *perfectly indifferent.*

<margin>*The source of love between the sexes.*</margin>

V. HERE we may observe the way in which NATURE leads *mankind* to the continuation of their race, and by its strongest power engages them in what causes the greatest toil and anxiety of life; and yet supports them under it with an inexpressible delight. We might have been encouraged toward the propagation of our species by such an uncomfortable sensation that it would have effectively compelled us to it, without any great prospect of happiness; just as we see *hunger* and *thirst* compel us to preserve our bodies, though few view eating and drinking as any significant happiness. The sexes might have been brought together, as we imagine the *animals* are, by *desire* alone, or by a *love* of *sensual pleasure*. But how dull and lifeless would life have been if there were nothing more in MARRIAGE? Who would have had enough determination to bear all the cares of a family and the education of *children*? Or who, from the general motive of *benevolence* alone, would have chosen to subject himself to *natural affection* toward *offspring*, when he could so easily foresee what troubles it might cause?

### Sect. 6. Approval and Gratitude toward the Person who puts us in such an *agreeable, innocent, good-natured,* and *easy* state of Mind, as we are conscious of while we enjoy *pleasant Conversation,* enlivened by *moderate Laughter.*

<margin>
The Power of Oratory founded on it.
</margin>

VI. UPON this *moral sense* is founded all the power of the ORATOR. The various figures of speech are the several ways which a lively genius, warmed with passions suitable to the occasion, naturally runs into, only a little diversified by custom: and they only move the hearers by giving a lively representation of the passions of the speaker; which are communicated to the hearers, as we * observed above regarding one passion, *namely Pity.*

Now the passions which the orator attempts to raise are all founded on *moral qualities.* All the bold *metaphors* or *descriptions,* all the artificial manners of expostulation, arguing, and addressing the audience, all the appeals to mankind, are but more lively methods of giving the *audience* a stronger impression of the *moral qualities* of the person accused or defended; of the action advised or discouraged: And all the *antitheses* or *witticisms*; all the cadences of sonorous periods, whatever

When we are trying to arouse any *desire* or *admiration* for an *object* that is truly beautiful, we are not satisfied with a mere narration, but endeavor, if we can, to present the *object* itself, or the most vivid *image* of it. And hence the *epic poem* or *tragedy* provides a vastly greater pleasure than the writings of *philosophers*, though both aim at recommending *virtue*. The representation of the actions themselves—if the representation is *judicious*, *natural*, and *vivid*—will make us admire the good and detest the *vicious*, the *inhuman*, the *treacherous*, and the *cruel*, by means of our moral *sense*, without any reflections from the *poet* to guide our sentiments. It is for this reason that HORACE has rightly made knowledge of *morals* so necessary for a good *poet*:

->*Scribendi recte* SAPERE *est and principium and fons*.*<-

<margin>
Imagery in poetry founded on the moral sense.

UPON this same sense is founded the power of that great beauty in poetry, the *PROSOPOPOEIA* <note>Personification, or the representation of an abstract quality or inanimate object as a person.</note>, by which every *affection* is made a *person*; every *natural event*, *cause*, and *object* is animated by *moral epithets*. For we join the contemplation of *moral circumstances* and *qualities* along with *natural objects* to increase their *beauty* or *deformity*; and we affect the hearer in a more lively manner with the affections described by representing them as *persons*. Thus, a shady wood must have its solemn, venerable *genius* <note>A guardian spirit or tutelary deity of a place.</note>, and proper *rural gods*; every clear fountain, its sacred, chaste *nymph*; and every river, its bountiful *god*, with his *urn*, and perhaps a *cornucopia* diffusing *plenty* and *fruitfulness* along its banks. The *daylight* is holy, benign, and powerful to banish the pernicious *spirits of the night*. <header>MORAL GOOD and EVIL. Sect. 6.</header>

EVERYONE perceives a greater beauty in this manner of representation, this imagery, this conjunction of *moral ideas*, than in the fullest narration or the most lively natural description. When one reads the fourth book of HOMER and is prepared, by the Council of the Gods, to imagine the bloody sequel, and amidst the most beautiful description ever imagined of *shooting an arrow*, meets with its *moral epithet*,

->—— μέλαιναων ἑρμ' ὀδυνάων \*,<-
->—— *The Source of blackest Woes*;<-

he will find himself more moved by this circumstance than by all the profusion of natural description that man could imagine.

VIII. HISTORY derives its chief excellence from the representing of *manners* and *characters*; the contemplation of which in *nature* being very affecting, they must necessarily give pleasure when well related.

<page-num>266</page-num>

<margin>
Section 7.
</margin>

---

# S E C T. VII.

## A Deduction of some Complex mo-
## ral Ideas, namely of Obligation, and
## Right, Perfect, Imperfect, and Ex-
## ternal, Alienable, and Unaliena-
## ble, from this moral Sense.

I. To conclude this subject, we may see, from what has been said, the true *origin* of moral ideas, namely: *This moral sense of excellence in every appearance, or evidence of benevolence.* It remains to be explained how we acquire more particular ideas of *virtue* and *vice*, independently of any *law, human* or *divine.*

<margin>
*Obligation.*
</margin>
If anyone asks, "Can we have any sense of OBLIGATION, independently of the *laws of a superior?*" we must answer according to the various senses of the word *obligation*. If by *obligation* we understand a *determination, without regard to our own interest, to approve actions and to perform them—a determination which shall also make us displeased with ourselves and uneasy after having acted contrary to it—* in this meaning of the word *obligation*,

<meta>catchword: there</meta>

<summary>This page introduces Section VII, which aims to derive complex moral concepts like obligation and rights from the innate moral sense. It begins by defining obligation as an internal motivation to act benevolently and the resulting self-disapproval when one fails to do so, regardless of external laws.</summary>

<page-num>267</page-num>
<header>MORAL GOOD and EVIL.</header>

<margin>Section 7.</margin>
there is *naturally* an *obligation* upon all men toward *benevolence*; and they are still under its influence, even when, by false or partial opinions of the natural tendency of their actions, this *moral sense* leads them to *evil*—unless by long, deep-rooted habits it is exceedingly weakened. For it hardly seems possible to wholly extinguish it. Or, which is to the same purpose, this *internal sense* and *instinct* toward *benevolence* will either influence our actions or else make us very uneasy and dissatisfied; and we shall be conscious that we are in a base, unhappy state, even without considering any *law* whatsoever, or any lost external advantages, or disadvantages impending from its sanctions. And further, there are still such indications given to us of what is benevolent on the whole, and what is not, as may probably reveal to us the true tendency of every action and let us see, at some time or another, the evil tendency of what, upon a partial view, appeared benevolent. Or, if we have no friends so faithful as to admonish us, the persons injured will not fail to reproach us. So no mortal can secure for himself a perpetual serenity, satisfaction, and self-approbation, except by a *serious inquiry* into the tendency of his actions and a perpetual study of the *universal good*, according to the most just notions of it.

But *virtue* itself, or *good dispositions* of mind, are not directly taught or produced by *instruction*; they must be originally implanted in our nature by its great AUTHOR, and afterwards strengthened and confirmed by our own cultivation.

<margin>Moral sense, not from reflection.</margin>
It is perhaps true that *reflection* and *reason* might lead us to approve the same *actions* as advantageous. But would not the same *reflection* and *reason* also generally recommend the same *foods* to us which our *taste* represents as pleasant? And shall we then conclude that we have no *sense* of *tasting*? Or that such a *sense* is *useless*? No; the use is plain in both cases. Despite the mighty *reason* we boast of above other *animals*, its processes are too slow, too full of doubt and hesitation, to serve us in every emergency—either for our own preservation without the external senses, or to direct our actions for the *good* of the *whole* without this *moral sense*. Nor could we be so strongly determined at all times toward what is most conducive to either of these ends without these prompt monitors and urgent solicitors; nor so nobly rewarded, when we act vigorously in pursuit of these ends, by the calm, dull reflections of *self-interest* as by those delightful sensations.

This natural *determination* to approve and admire, or hate and dislike actions, is no doubt an *occult quality*. But

<summary>The author argues that human reason is too slow and hesitant to guide conduct effectively without the immediate prompts of a moral sense, comparing it to how the sense of taste guides us more effectively than nutritional theory. He asserts that the delightful sensations of the moral sense provide a much stronger motivation for the common good than the dull reflections of self-interest.</summary>

### Sect. 7.

the delightful *consciousness* which arises from the *moral sense*. The reader hardly needs to be told that by *constraint*, we do not mean an *external force* moving our limbs without our consent—for in that case, we are not *agents* at all—but rather that *constraint* which arises from the threatening and presenting of some *evil* in order to make us act in a certain manner. And yet, there seems to be a universally acknowledged difference between even this sort of *constraint* and *obligation*. We never say we are *obliged* to do an *action* which we count base, but we may be *constrained* to it; we never say that the *divine laws*, by their *sanctions*, *constrain* us, but *oblige* us; nor do we call obedience to the DEITY *constraint*, unless by a *metaphor*, though many admit they are influenced by fear of *punishments*. And yet, supposing an almighty *evil being* should require, under grievous *penalties*, *treachery*, *cruelty*, and *ingratitude*, we would call this *constraint*. The difference is plainly this: when any *sanctions* cooperate with our *moral sense* in exciting us to *actions* which we count *morally good*, we say we are *obliged*; but when *sanctions* of *rewards* or *punishments* oppose our *moral sense*, then we say we are *bribed* or *constrained*. In the former case, we call the *lawgiver* good, as designing the *public happiness*; in the latter, we call him *evil* or *unjust* for the supposed contrary

He notes that the term "obligation" implies a lawgiver who intends the public happiness, while "constraint" suggests an unjust or evil authority.</summary>

<page-num>277</page-num>
<header>MORAL GOOD and EVIL.</header>

Intention. But if all our ideas of *moral* <margin>Sect. 7.</margin> good or evil were derived solely from opinions of *private advantage* or *loss* in *actions*, I see no possible difference that could be made in the meaning of these words.

VI. FROM this sense too, we derive our *rights*. Ideas of RIGHTS. Whenever it appears to us that *a capacity for doing, demanding, or possessing anything, universally allowed in certain circumstances, would on the whole tend toward the general good*, we say that any person in such circumstances has a *right* to do, possess, or demand that *thing*. And according as this tendency toward the public good is greater or less, the *right* is greater or less.

<margin>*Perfect Rights.*</margin>
THE rights called *perfect* are of such necessity to the public good that the *universal violation of them would make human life intolerable*; and it actually makes those miserable whose *rights* are thus violated. On the contrary, to fulfill these *rights* in every instance tends toward the public good, either directly or by promoting the innocent advantage of a *part*. Hence it plainly follows, "that to allow a violent defense or enforcement of such *rights*, before civil government is established, cannot in any particular case be more detrimental to the public than the violation of them with impunity."

<sig>T 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: And</meta>

<summary>The author defines "rights" as powers or claims that tend toward the general good and distinguishes "perfect rights" as those essential to the very survival and tolerability of human society.</summary>
<keywords>moral good, moral evil, private advantage, rights, public good, perfect rights, civil government, social contract</keywords>

--- Page 312 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on rights, specifically detailing how the violation of "perfect rights" justifies the use of force and serves as the foundation for the magistrate's authority to punish in a civil state.</meta>

<page-num>278</page-num>
<header>An INQUIRY concerning</header>
<meta>catchword: IMPER-</meta>

Sect. 7.
---
And as for the general consequences, the universal use of force in a *state of nature*, in pursuit of *perfect rights*, seems exceedingly advantageous to the *whole*, by making everyone dread any attempts against the *perfect rights* of others.

<margin>*Right of war and punishment.*</margin>
THIS is the *moral effect* which attends *actual injury*, or a violation of the *perfect rights* of others—namely, a right to *war* and all violence which is necessary to force the *injurer* to repair the damage and provide security against such offenses for the future. This is the sole foundation of the right to punish criminals and of the violent enforcement of our rights in a *state of nature*. And these rights—belonging originally to the persons injured, or their voluntary or invited assistants, according to the judgment of impartial arbitrators in a *state of nature*—being transferred by the consent of the injured persons to the magistrate in a *civil state*, are the true foundation of his right of punishment. Instances of *perfect rights* include the rights to our *lives*; to the *fruits* of our *labors*; to demand the performance of *contracts* based on valuable considerations from men capable of performing them; to *direct* our own *actions* for either public or innocent private good before we have submitted them to the direction of others in any measure; and many others of a similar nature.

<summary>Hutcheson explains that the right to use force to protect "perfect rights" in a state of nature is the foundation for the legal right to punish and wage war. He defines perfect rights as those concerning life, labor, and contracts, which are transferred to the magistrate upon entering a civil state.</summary>
<keywords>state of nature, perfect rights, right of war, punishment, civil state, magistrate, contracts, labor, impartiality, moral effect</keywords>

--- Page 313 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the definition of perfect rights and the justification of force to the definition of imperfect rights, which do not justify the use of force.</meta>

<page-num>279</page-num>
<header>MORAL GOOD and EVIL. Sect. 7.</header>

<margin>
~~~
Imperfect Rights.
~~~
</margin>

IMPERFECT *rights* are those which, when universally violated, would not necessarily make men miserable. These *rights* tend toward the improvement and increase of *positive good* in any society, but are not absolutely necessary to prevent *universal misery*. The violation of them only disappoints men of the happiness expected from the *humanity* or *gratitude* of others, but does not deprive men of any good which they had before. From this description it appears, “*that a violent pursuit of such* *rights would generally cause greater* *evil than the violation of them*.” Besides, the allowing of force in such cases would deprive men of the greatest pleasure in actions of *kindness*, *humanity*, and *gratitude*; which would cease to appear admirable when men could be constrained to perform them. Instances of *imperfect rights* are those which the *poor* have to the *charity* of the *wealthy*; which all *men* have to *favors* <note>The original uses "Offices" in the sense of helpful duties or kind acts.</note> of no trouble or expense to the *performer*; which *benefactors* have to returns of *gratitude*, and such like.

THE violation of *imperfect rights* only suggests that a man has such weak *benevolence* that he does not study advancing the *positive good* of others when it is in the least opposite to his own; but the violation of per-

<sig>T 4</sig>
<meta>catchword: fect</meta>

<summary>Hutcheson defines imperfect rights as those which improve society but do not justify the use of force, such as charity or gratitude. He explains that enforcing these rights through violence would create more evil than their violation and would destroy the moral value of kind actions.</summary>
<keywords>imperfect rights, positive good, benevolence, charity, gratitude, moral philosophy, use of force, humanity</keywords>

--- Page 314 ---
<meta>This page continues the comparison between perfect and imperfect rights and introduces the concept of external rights.</meta>

<page-num>280</page-num>
<header>An INQUIRY concerning</header>

<margin>Sect. 7.</margin> fect *rights*, argues the injurious *person* to be positively evil or cruel; or at least so immoderately *selfish* as to be indifferent about the *positive misery* and *ruin* of others, when he imagines he can find his *interest* in it. In violating the former, we show a weak *desire* for *public happiness*, which every small *view* of *private interest* overbalances; but in violating the latter, we show ourselves so entirely negligent of the *misery* of others, that *views* of increasing our own *good* overcome all our *compassion* toward their *sufferings*. Now as the absence of *good* is more easily borne than the presence of *misery*, so our *good wishes* toward the *positive good* of others are weaker than our *compassion* toward their *misery*. He then who violates *imperfect rights* shows that his *self-love* overcomes only the *desire* of *positive good* to others; but he who violates *perfect rights* betrays such a *selfish desire* of advancing his own *positive good* as overcomes all *compassion* toward the *misery* of others.

<margin>External Rights.</margin> BESIDE these two sorts of *rights*, there is a third called *external*; as when the doing, possessing, or demanding of anything is really detrimental to the *public* in any particular *instance*, as being contrary to the *imperfect right* of another; but yet the *universally* denying *men* this *faculty* <note>In this context, "faculty" refers to a legal or moral power or right to act.</note>

<summary>Hutcheson explains that violating perfect rights demonstrates a cruel indifference to the misery of others, whereas violating imperfect rights merely shows a weak desire for their positive happiness. He also introduces external rights, which are actions or claims that might be harmful in specific cases but must be generally permitted.</summary>
<keywords>perfect rights, imperfect rights, external rights, self-love, compassion, public happiness, misery, moral philosophy</keywords>

<margin>Sect. 7.</margin>
of doing, possessing, or demanding that thing, or of using force in pursuit of it, would do more mischief than all the evils to be feared from the use of this faculty. And hence it appears,

> “ force in pursuit of them.”

**CIVIL** societies substitute *actions in law* instead of the force allowed in the *state of nature*.

**INSTANCES** of external *rights* are these: that of a wealthy *miser* to recall his loan from the most industrious poor tradesman at any time; that of demanding the performance of a *covenant* too burdensome on one side; the right of a wealthy heir to refuse payment of any debts which were contracted by him while under age, without fraud in the lender; the right of taking advantage of a *positive law* contrary to what was *equity* prior to that law; as when a *registered deed* takes precedence over one not registered, although prior to it and known to be so before the second contract.

<margin>What rights can be opposite.</margin>
Now, whereas no *action*, demand, or *possession* can at once be either necessary to the public good or conducive to it, and

<summary>Hutcheson argues that force should not be used against external rights because the resulting social disorder would outweigh any benefits. He provides examples of these rights, such as a miser's legal claim on a loan or the enforcement of technically valid but inequitable contracts.</summary>

### Sect. 7. Instances to show the use of the two marks of alienable rights, which must both concur to make them so, and will explain the manner of applying them in other cases.

<margin>
The foundation of property.
</margin>

VIII. THAT we may see the foundation of some of the more important rights of mankind, let us observe that probably nine-tenths, at least, of the things which are useful to mankind are owing to their *labor* and *industry*; and consequently, once men become so numerous that the *natural product* of the earth is not sufficient for their support, or ease, or innocent pleasure, a necessity arises, for the support of the increasing *system*, that such a *tenor* of conduct be observed as shall most effectively promote *industry*; and that men abstain from all actions which would have the contrary effect. It is well known that *general benevolence* alone is not a motive strong enough for *industry*, to bear *labor* and *toil* and many other difficulties to which we are averse due to *self-love*. For the strengthening of our motives for *industry*, therefore, we have the strongest attractions of *blood*, of *friendship*, of *gratitude*, and the additional motives of *honor*, and even of *external interest*. *Self-love* is really as necessary to the good of the *whole* as *benevolence*; just as that attraction which causes the cohesion of the parts is as necessary to the *regular*

<meta>catchword: State</meta>

<summary>Hutcheson argues that property rights are founded on labor and industry, which are necessary when natural resources become insufficient for a growing population. He asserts that self-love and private interests are essential supplements to general benevolence in motivating the hard work required to sustain society.</summary>
<keywords>foundation of property, labor, industry, self-love, benevolence, economic necessity, social system</keywords>

This is the *Ground* of our *Right* of *Dominion* and *Property* in the *Fruits* of our *Labours*; without which *Right*, we could scarce hope for any *Industry*, or any thing beyond the *Product* of uncultivated *Nature*. *Industry* will be confined to our present *Necessities*, and cease when they are provided for; at least it will only continue from the weak *Motive* of general *Benevolence*, if we are not allowed to store up beyond present *Necessity*, and to dispose of what is above our *Necessities*,

Besides, there is no determining in many cases who is a *miser* and who is not.

<page-num>287</page-num>
<header>MORAL GOOD and EVIL. Sect. 7.</header>

<margin>
Commerce.
</margin>

THE labor of each man cannot furnish him with all necessities, though it may furnish him with a needless plenty of one sort: hence the *right of commerce*, and alienating our goods; and also the *rights from contracts and promises*, either to the goods acquired by others, or to their *labors*.

<margin>
Right of Civil Government.
</margin>

THE great advantages which accrue to mankind from unprejudiced *arbitrators*, empowered to decide the *controversies* which ordinarily arise among neighbors through the partiality of *self-love*; as also from prudent *directors*, who should not only instruct the multitude in the best methods of promoting the *public good* and of defending themselves against mutual or foreign *injuries*, but also be armed with force sufficient to make their decrees or orders effective at home and the society formidable abroad—these advantages, I say, sufficiently show the *right* men have to constitute *civil government* and to subject their *alienable rights* to the disposal of their governors, under such *limitations* as their prudence suggests. And as far as the people have subjected their rights, so far their governors have an external *right* at least to dispose of them, as their prudence shall direct, for attaining the ends of their institution, and no further.

### Sect. 7.

<margin>Corollaries for comparing the degrees of virtue and vice in actions.</margin>
IX. These instances may show how our *moral sense*, by a little reflection upon the tendencies of actions, may adjust the *rights of mankind*. Let us now apply the general canon laid down above <note>See Section 3, Articles 11, 12.</note>, for comparing the degrees of *virtue* and *vice* in actions, in a few corollaries besides that one already deduced <note>See Section 3, Article 15, Paragraph 3.</note>.

<margin>From ability.</margin>
1. The disappointment, in whole or in part, of any attempt, *good* or *evil*, if it is occasioned only by external force or any unforeseen accident, does not vary the *moral good* or *evil*. For as in *good* attempts, the *moment of good*, or [M], is diminished or vanishes in such a case, so does the *ability*, or [A], likewise: the quotient then may still be the same. This holds equally in *evil* attempts. So that actions are not to be judged *good* or *evil* by their outcomes, any further than they might have been foreseen by the *agent* in *evil* attempts, or were actually intended, if they were *good*, in *good* actions; for then only they demonstrate either *love* or *hatred* in the *agent*.

<margin>Interest.</margin>
2. Secular rewards attached to *virtue*, and actually influencing the *agent* fur-

In this, however, no *mortal* is capable of judging another. Nor do the *prospects* of *grateful returns* for *benefits* which we would have conferred gratuitously, diminish the *generosity*. ### Sect. 7.

7. **DUTIES** of no *Toil* or *Expense* generally have little *Virtue*, because the *Ability* to perform them is very great, and there is no *contrary Interest* to be overcome.

8. BUT the refusing of them may be very vicious, as it argues an absence of good *Affection*, and often produces a great enough *measure* <note>"Moment" in the original, referring to the weight or quantity of an effect.</note> of *natural Evil*. And,

<margin>Degree of Right.</margin>
9. IN general, the fulfilling of the *perfect Rights* of others has little *Virtue* in it; for by doing so, no more *measure of Good* is produced than there was before; and the *Interest* motivating the *Action* is very great—even the avoiding of all the Evils of *War* in a *State of Nature*.

10. BUT the violating of *perfect*, or even *external Rights*, is always exceedingly evil, either in the immediate or more remote *Consequences* of the *Action*; and the *selfish Motives* overcome by this vicious *Inclination* are the same as those in the former *Case*.

11. THE truest *Matter of Praise* are those *Actions* or *Duties* which others claim from us by an *imperfect Right*; and generally, the *stronger* their *Right* is, the *less Virtue* there is in fulfilling it, but the *greater Vice* in violating it.

<summary>This page analyzes how the strength of a "right" affects moral judgment, arguing that fulfilling basic legal or "perfect" rights carries little virtue while violating them is highly wicked. True praise is reserved for fulfilling "imperfect rights," where benevolent actions are not strictly compelled by the threat of conflict.</summary>

<margin>
Sect. 7.
Strength of Ties.
</margin>

Lemma. The stronger ties of *benevolence*, given equal *abilities*, must produce a greater *measure* <note>"Moment" in the original, referring to the weight, importance, or quantity of an effect.</note> of *good*, in equally good *characters*, than the weaker ties. Thus, *natural affection*, *gratitude*, and *friendship* have greater *effects* than *general benevolence*. *Hence*,

12. In equal *measures* of *good* produced by two *agents*, when one acts from *general benevolence* and the other from a *nearer tie*, there is greater *virtue* in the *agent* who produces equal *good* from the weaker attachment, and less *virtue* where there is the stronger attachment which yet produces no more.

13. But the omission of the *good deeds* of the stronger ties, or *actions* contrary to them, have greater *vice* in them than the like omissions or *actions* contrary to the weaker ties; since our *selfishness* or *malice* must appear the greater by the strength of the contrary attachment which it overcomes. Thus, in cooperating with *gratitude*, *natural affection*, or *friendship*, we evidence less *virtue* in any given *measure* of *good* produced than in equally important *actions* of *general benevolence*. But *ingratitude* to a *benefactor*, *negligence* of the *interests* of a *friend* or *relative*, or returns of evil *deeds* are vastly more odious,

<summary>This page argues that performing a good deed out of general benevolence is more virtuous than doing it for a friend, as the latter is driven by a stronger natural pull. However, violating close ties like friendship or family is considered a much greater vice because it requires a higher degree of selfishness to overcome those natural bonds.</summary>

<margin>What duties are to be preferred, when there appears any opposition.</margin>
14. WHEN we cannot at once follow two different inclinations of *benevolence*, we are to prefer gratifying the stronger inclination; according to the wise order of NATURE, who has constituted these attachments. Thus, we are rather to be *grateful* than *liberal*, and rather serve a *friend* or *kinsman* than a *stranger* of only equal *virtue*, when we cannot do both.

15. OR more generally, since there can be no *right*, *claim*, or *obligation* toward *impossibilities*; when two *actions* to be done by any agent would both tend to the good of mankind, but they cannot both be performed at once; that which produces the most *good* is to be done, if the omission of the other produces no outweighing <note>"Prepollent" in the original; meaning superior in weight or power.</note> *evil*. If the omission of either will cause some *new natural evil*, that action is to be omitted whose omission will cause the least *evil*. Thus, if two persons of unequal dignity be in danger, we are to relieve the more valuable one when we cannot relieve both. *Ingratitude*, as it evidences a worse temperament than the neglect of *beneficence*, so it raises worse sentiments in the benefactor, and greater distrust <note>"Diffidence" in the original, here meaning a lack of confidence or trust in others.</note> and suspicion of his

If omitting one action will occasion *new positive evil*, or the continuance of a *state of pain*, whereas the omission of another would only prevent some *new positive good*; since a *state of pain* is a greater *evil* than the absence of *good*, we are to follow *compassion* rather than *kindness*; and relieve the *distressed* rather than increase the *pleasures* of the *comfortable* <note>"Easy" in the original, meaning those living in comfort or without distress.</note>; when we cannot do both at once, and other circumstances of the objects are equal. <meta>This page continues the discussion of the limits of government power, specifically detailing the conditions under which subjects are morally restrained from exercising their right to resistance and defining the nature of absolute government.</meta>

<page-num>295</page-num>
<header>MORAL GOOD and EVIL.</header>
<sig>U 4</sig>
<meta>catchword: when-</meta>

<margin>Section 7.</margin>
The only moral restraints upon subjects in such cases are when they foresee that, through their lack of force, they shall probably, by resistance, cause greater evils to the public than those they attempt to remove; or when they find that governors, who are generally very useful to the public, have, through some ill-advised passion, done an injury too small to outweigh the advantages of their administration or the evils which resistance would in all likelihood cause—especially when the injury is of a private nature and not likely to be made a precedent for the ruin of others. *Unalienable rights are essential limitations in all governments.*

<margin>Absolute Government.</margin>
*But* by absolute government—whether in a prince, a council, or both jointly—we understand a right to dispose of the natural force and goods of a whole people, as far as they are naturally alienable, according to the prudence of the prince, the council, or both jointly, for the public good of the state or the whole people, without any reservation as to the quantity of the goods, the manner of levying them, or the proportion of the subjects' labor which they shall demand. But in all states this tacit trust is presupposed:

> "that the power conferred shall be employed according to the best judgment of the rulers for the public good."

So that

<vocab>moral restraints, resistance, absolute government, public good, tacit trust, unalienable rights, subjects, governors</vocab>

<summary>This page outlines the moral conditions that discourage subjects from resisting authority, such as the risk of greater public harm, and defines absolute government as a trust where rulers manage a people's alienable rights for the common good.</summary>

<page-num>296</page-num>
<header>An INQUIRY concerning</header>

### Sect. 7.

Whenever the governors openly profess a design of destroying the state, or act in such a manner as will necessarily do so, the essential trust supposed in all transfer of *civil power* is violated, and the grant is thereby made void.

<margin>Limited Government.</margin>
A prince, a council, or both jointly, may be limited in various ways: either when the consent of the one is necessary for the validity of the acts of the other; or when, in the very constitution of this supreme power, certain affairs are expressly exempted from the jurisdiction of the prince, the council, or both jointly. This occurs, for example, when several independent states unite to form a general council, from whose authority they expressly reserve certain privileges during the very formation of this council; or when, in the very constitution of any state, a certain method for the election of the prince or of the members of the supreme council is determined, and the intention of their assembly is declared. In all such cases, it is not within the power of such a prince, council, or both jointly, to alter the form of government itself, or to take away that right which the people have to be governed in such a manner by a prince or council thus elected, without the universal consent of the very people who have subjected themselves to this form of

<meta>catchword: Govern-</meta>

<summary>This section argues that a government's authority is voided if it acts to destroy the state, and it defines limited government as being restricted by constitutional exemptions, shared consent, or specific electoral methods.</summary>
<keywords>civil power, limited government, state destruction, constitution, jurisdiction, election of rulers, social contract</keywords>

government. So that there may be a very regular state where there is no universal absolute power held either by one person, a council, or by any other assembly besides that of the whole people united into that state. To say that if the *supreme power* attempts a change in the very *form of the government*, the people have no remedy according to the constitution itself, will not prove that the supreme power has such a *right*—unless we confuse all ideas of *right* with those of *external force*. Indeed, the only remedy in that case is a universal insurrection against such *perfidious trustees*.

<margin>The nature of despotic power.</margin>
**DESPOTIC POWER** is that which *injured persons may acquire over those criminals whose lives they may prolong, consistent with public safety, so that through their labor they may repair the damages they have done; or over those who are indebted for a greater value than all their goods and labor can possibly equal.* This power itself is limited only to the goods and labor of the criminals or debtors; it includes no right to torture, prostitution, or any rights of the governed which are naturally unalienable; nor does it include a right to anything that is not of some importance toward the repair of damage, the payment of debt, or security against future offenses. <summary>This page argues that a legitimate state does not require a single absolute ruler and that the people have a right to insurrection if their leaders betray their trust. It also defines despotic power as a limited right to an offender's labor for reparations, which does not extend to violating unalienable human rights.</summary>

And therefore in cases of extreme *necessity*, when the state cannot otherwise be preserved from ruin, it must certainly be *just* and *good* in limited governors, or in any other persons who can do it, to use the force of the state for its own preservation, beyond the limits fixed by the *constitution*, in some *transitory acts*, which are not to be made *precedents*. And on the other hand, when an equal *necessity* to avoid ruin requires it, the subjects may justly resume the powers ordinarily lodged in their governors, or may counteract them. This privilege of *flagrant necessity* we all allow in defense of the most perfect *private rights*: and if *public rights* are of more extensive importance, so also are public *necessities*. These *necessities* must be very grievous and flagrant; otherwise they can never overbalance the *evils* of violating

No *person* or *state* can be happy where they do not think their *important rights* are secured from the *cruelty*, *avarice*, *ambition*, or *caprice* of their governors. ### Sect. 7.
---

“goodness, or any other *quality*, to give *laws* to others without their *consent*, express or tacit; or to dispose of the *fruits* of their *labors*, or of any other *right* whatsoever.” And therefore *superior wisdom*, or *goodness*, gives no *right* to men to govern others.

<margin>Divine government founded on wisdom and goodness.</margin>
But then with relation to the DEITY, supposed omniscient and benevolent, and secure from *neediness*, the ordinary cause of *injuries* toward others; it must be *amiable* in such a *Being* to assume the government of weak, inconstant creatures, often misled by *selfishness*, and to give them laws. To these laws every mortal should submit out of *public love*, as being contrived for the *good of the whole*, and for the greatest *private good* consistent with it; and everyone may be sure that he shall be better directed how to attain these ends by the *divine laws* than by his own greatest prudence and circumspection. Hence we imagine, “That a good and wise GOD must have a perfect *right* to govern the *universe*; and that all mortals are obliged to *universal obedience*.”

<margin>What divine justice is.</margin>
The justice of the DEITY is only a conception of his *universal impartial benevolence*, as it shall influence him, if he gives any laws, to temper them to the *universal good*, and enforce them with the

<margin>Creation is not the basis of God's dominion.</margin>
XI. Some imagine that the *property* the Creator has in all his works must be the true foundation of his *right* to govern. Among men indeed, we find it necessary for the public good that none should arbitrarily dispose of the goods acquired by the labor of another, which we call his *property*; and hence we imagine that creation is the *only foundation* of God's dominion. But if the reason <note>See Art. 10. Par. 6. of this section.</note> for establishing the *rights of property* does not hold against a *perfectly wise and benevolent Being*, I see no reason why *property* should be necessary to his dominion. Now the reason does not hold: for an infinitely wise and good Being could never employ his assumed authority to counteract the *universal good*. The tie of gratitude is stronger indeed than bare benevolence; and therefore, supposing two equally wise and good beings, the one our *Creator* and the other not, we should think ourselves more obliged to obey our *Creator*. But supposing our *Creator* were malicious, and a good being condescended to rescue us, or govern us better, with sufficient power to accomplish his kind intentions; his *right* to govern would be perfectly good. But

Sect. 7. This is rather a matter of curious *speculation* than *use*; since both titles of *benevolence* and *property* coincide in the *one only true* DEITY, as far as we can know, joined with infinite *wisdom* and *power*.

<margin>Our *moral sense* is the *effect* of *divine goodness*.</margin>
XII. IF it is here asked, "Could not the DEITY have given us a different or contrary determination of *mind*, namely, to approve *actions* upon another *foundation* than *benevolence*?" It is certain that there is nothing in this surpassing the natural power of the DEITY. But as in the first *Treatise* \*, we attributed the constitution of our present *sense* of *beauty* to divine *goodness*, so with much more obvious reason may we ascribe the present constitution of our *moral sense* to his *goodness*. For if the DEITY is really *benevolent*, or delights in the happiness of others, he could not rationally act otherwise, or give us a *moral sense* upon another foundation, without counteracting his own benevolent intentions. For, even upon the supposition of a contrary *sense*, every rational *being* must still have been concerned in some degree about his own external happiness: *reflection* on the circumstances of mankind in this world would have suggested, that *universal benevolence* and a *social temperament*, or a certain course of external *actions*,

<margin>
Whence this universal opinion of the Divine Goodness.
</margin>
XIII. IT has often been taken for granted in these *papers*, "That the DEITY is *morally good*"; though the reasoning is not at all built upon this supposition. If we inquire into the reason for the great agreement of mankind in this opinion, we shall perhaps find no demonstrative arguments *a priori*, from the idea of an *Independent Being*, to prove his goodness. But there is abundant probability, deduced from the whole *frame of nature*, which seems, as far as we know, plainly contrived for the good of the *whole*; and the incidental evils seem the necessary concomitants of some mechanism designed for vastly preponderant good. Nay, this very *moral sense*, implanted in rational agents, to delight in and admire whatever actions flow from a study

<meta>This page concludes the inquiry by arguing that the belief in the Deity's goodness stems from the observation of the world's design and the logical conclusion that a perfect being finds happiness in universal benevolence.</meta>

<page-num>304</page-num>
<header>An INQUIRY, &c.</header>

## Sect. 7. Study of the *Good of others*, is one of the strongest Evidences of Goodness in the AUTHOR of Nature.

---

But these reflections are in no way as universal as the *opinion*, nor are they often taught by anyone. What leads *mankind* more probably to that *opinion* then, is this: the obvious *structure of the world* gives us ideas of *boundless wisdom* and *power* in its AUTHOR. We cannot conceive of such a *Being* as *needy*, and must conclude that he is *happy* and in the *best state possible*, since he can always satisfy himself. The *best state of rational agents*, and their *greatest and most worthy happiness*, we are necessarily led to imagine must consist in *universal effective benevolence*; and hence we conclude that the DEITY is benevolent in the *most universal and impartial manner*. Nor can we easily imagine what else deserves the name of *perfection* except for *benevolence*, and those *capacities* or *abilities* which are necessary to make it *effective*, such as *wisdom* and *power*; at least we can have no other valuable conception of it.

->FINIS.<-

<summary>The author argues that mankind identifies God as benevolent because a perfect, self-sufficient being would naturally find the highest form of happiness in universal benevolence. This conclusion is supported by the evidence of boundless wisdom and power seen in the design of the universe.</summary>
<keywords>Deity, benevolence, Author of Nature, perfection, happiness, wisdom, power, rational agents, structure of the world</keywords>
